=== Ch 1: Preface ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I have carefully collected whatever I have been able to learn of the story of poor Werther, and here present it to you, knowing that you will thank me for it. To his spirit and character you cannot refuse your admiration and love: to his fate you will not deny your tears.
CAN: I have gathered with care everything I could learn about the story of poor Werther, and I set it before you here, certain that you will thank me for it. You cannot withhold your admiration and love from his spirit and character, nor your tears from his fate.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: And thou, good soul, who sufferest the same distress as he endured once, draw comfort from his sorrows; and let this little book be thy friend, if, owing to fortune or through thine own fault, thou canst not find a dearer companion.
CAN: And you, good soul, feeling the same anguish that once pressed on him — take comfort from his sorrows, and let this little book be your friend, if fate or your own fault leaves you no closer companion.

=== Ch 2: May 4, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: How happy I am that I am gone! My dear friend, what a thing is the heart of man! To leave you, from whom I have been inseparable, whom I love so dearly, and yet to feel happy! I know you will forgive me. Have not other attachments been specially appointed by fate to torment a head like mine? Poor Leonora! and yet I was not to blame. Was it my fault, that, whilst the peculiar charms of her sister afforded me an agreeable entertainment, a passion for me was engendered in her feeble heart? And yet am I wholly blameless? Did I not encourage her emotions? Did I not feel charmed at those truly genuine expressions of nature, which, though but little mirthful in reality, so often amused us? Did I not—but oh! what is man, that he dares so to accuse himself? My dear friend I promise you I will improve; I will no longer, as has ever been my habit, continue to ruminate on every petty vexation which fortune may dispense; I will enjoy the present, and the past shall be for me the past. No doubt you are right, my best of friends, there would be far less suffering amongst mankind, if men—and God knows why they are so fashioned—did not employ their imaginations so assiduously in recalling the memory of past sorrow, instead of bearing their present lot with equanimity. Be kind enough to inform my mother that I shall attend to her business to the best of my ability, and shall give her the earliest information about it. I have seen my aunt, and find that she is very far from being the disagreeable person our friends allege her to be. She is a lively, cheerful woman, with the best of hearts. I explained to her my mother's wrongs with regard to that part of her portion which has been withheld from her. She told me the motives and reasons of her own conduct, and the terms on which she is willing to give up the whole, and to do more than we have asked. In short, I cannot write further upon this subject at present; only assure my mother that all will go on well. And I have again observed, my dear friend, in this trifling affair, that misunderstandings and neglect occasion more mischief in the world than even malice and wickedness. At all events, the two latter are of less frequent occurrence.
CAN: How glad I am to be away! Dear friend, what a strange thing the human heart is! To leave you — you whom I have been inseparable from, whom I love so dearly — and still to feel happy! I know you will forgive me. Weren't my other attachments practically appointed by fate to torment a mind like mine? Poor Leonora! And yet I was not to blame. Was it my fault that, while her sister's particular charms gave me such pleasant company, a passion took root in her own fragile heart? And yet — am I entirely innocent? Didn't I encourage her feelings? Didn't I delight in those wholly genuine expressions of nature that, though hardly comic in themselves, so often made us laugh? Didn't I — oh, what is a man, that he dares accuse himself like this? I promise you, dear friend, I will do better; I will no longer chew over every small misfortune that fate deals out, the way I always have; I will enjoy the present, and let the past stay past. You are right, of course, my best of friends — there would be far less suffering among us if people didn't work their imaginations so hard recalling old griefs, instead of bearing the present with some composure, though God knows why we are made this way. Please tell my mother I will handle her business as well as I can and send her word of it as soon as possible. I have seen my aunt, and she is nothing like the disagreeable woman our relatives make her out to be. She is lively and warm, with the kindest heart. I explained my mother's grievance about the part of her share that has been kept back. My aunt told me her own reasons and motives, and the terms on which she is willing to hand over the whole of it — and to do more than we asked. I can't write any more about it just now; only assure my mother that it will all come right. And once again, dear friend, this small affair has shown me that misunderstanding and neglect cause more trouble in the world than even malice and cruelty do. At least the last two are rarer.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: In other respects I am very well off here. Solitude in this terrestrial paradise is a genial balm to my mind, and the young spring cheers with its bounteous promises my oftentimes misgiving heart. Every tree, every bush, is full of flowers; and one might wish himself transformed into a butterfly, to float about in this ocean of perfume, and find his whole existence in it.
CAN: Otherwise I am very well off here. Solitude in this earthly paradise is a soothing balm to my spirit, and this young spring warms my often anxious heart with all its generous promise. Every tree, every bush is a mass of blossom; you could wish yourself a butterfly, free to drift through this ocean of fragrance and find your whole life in it.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: The town itself is disagreeable; but then, all around, you find an inexpressible beauty of nature. This induced the late Count M to lay out a garden on one of the sloping hills which here intersect each other with the most charming variety, and form the most lovely valleys. The garden is simple; and it is easy to perceive, even upon your first entrance, that the plan was not designed by a scientific gardener, but by a man who wished to give himself up here to the enjoyment of his own sensitive heart. Many a tear have I already shed to the memory of its departed master in a summer-house which is now reduced to ruins, but was his favourite resort, and now is mine. I shall soon be master of the place. The gardener has become attached to me within the last few days, and he will lose nothing thereby.
CAN: The town itself is unpleasant, but all around it lies an indescribable natural beauty. That is what moved the late Count M to lay out a garden on one of the sloping hills that cross each other here in the loveliest variety, forming the most charming valleys. The garden is simple, and the moment you enter you can tell it was not designed by a trained gardener but by a man who wanted a place to give himself up to his own feeling heart. I have already shed many a tear for its dead master in a summer-house that is now falling to ruin — his favorite spot, and now mine. Soon I will be master of the place myself. The gardener has taken to me these last few days, and he won't be the worse for it.

=== Ch 3: May 10, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: A wonderful serenity has taken possession of my entire soul, like these sweet mornings of spring which I enjoy with my whole heart. I am alone, and feel the charm of existence in this spot, which was created for the bliss of souls like mine. I am so happy, my dear friend, so absorbed in the exquisite sense of mere tranquil existence, that I neglect my talents. I should be incapable of drawing a single stroke at the present moment; and yet I feel that I never was a greater artist than now. When, while the lovely valley teems with vapour around me, and the meridian sun strikes the upper surface of the impenetrable foliage of my trees, and but a few stray gleams steal into the inner sanctuary, I throw myself down among the tall grass by the trickling stream; and, as I lie close to the earth, a thousand unknown plants are noticed by me: when I hear the buzz of the little world among the stalks, and grow familiar with the countless indescribable forms of the insects and flies, then I feel the presence of the Almighty, who formed us in his own image, and the breath of that universal love which bears and sustains us, as it floats around us in an eternity of bliss; and then, my friend, when darkness overspreads my eyes, and heaven and earth seem to dwell in my soul and absorb its power, like the form of a beloved mistress, then I often think with longing, Oh, would I could describe these conceptions, could impress upon paper all that is living so full and warm within me, that it might be the mirror of my soul, as my soul is the mirror of the infinite God! O my friend—but it is too much for my strength—I sink under the weight of the splendour of these visions!
CAN: A wonderful calm has taken over my whole soul, like these sweet spring mornings that I enjoy with all my heart. I am alone, and glad to be alive in this place, which was made for souls like mine. I am so happy, dear friend, so lost in the sheer sense of quiet existence, that my art suffers for it. I couldn't draw a single line right now — and yet I have never been a greater painter than I am at this moment. When the lovely valley steams with mist around me, and the high midday sun rests on the surface of the dense canopy of my trees while only a few stray beams slip into the inner sanctuary, and I lie down in the tall grass beside the trickling stream — then, close to the earth, I notice a thousand small plants I had never seen; when I hear the hum of that little world among the stems and grow familiar with the countless strange shapes of the insects and the flies, then I feel the presence of the Almighty who made us in his own image, the breath of that all-embracing love that holds and carries us as it floats around us in an eternity of bliss. My friend! When the light then fails in my eyes, and the world around me and the sky above come to rest wholly in my soul like the face of a woman I love, I am often seized with longing, and think: oh, if only I could express this, could breathe onto paper all that lives so full and warm inside me, so that it might mirror my soul as my soul mirrors the infinite God! My friend — but it is beyond me; I go under, crushed by the sheer power of these visions.

=== Ch 4: May 12, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I know not whether some deceitful spirits haunt this spot, or whether it be the warm, celestial fancy in my own heart which makes everything around me seem like paradise. In front of the house is a fountain,—a fountain to which I am bound by a charm like Melusina and her sisters. Descending a gentle slope, you come to an arch, where, some twenty steps lower down, water of the clearest crystal gushes from the marble rock. The narrow wall which encloses it above, the tall trees which encircle the spot, and the coolness of the place itself,—everything imparts a pleasant but sublime impression. Not a day passes on which I do not spend an hour there. The young maidens come from the town to fetch water,—innocent and necessary employment, and formerly the occupation of the daughters of kings. As I take my rest there, the idea of the old patriarchal life is awakened around me. I see them, our old ancestors, how they formed their friendships and contracted alliances at the fountain-side; and I feel how fountains and streams were guarded by beneficent spirits. He who is a stranger to these sensations has never really enjoyed cool repose at the side of a fountain after the fatigue of a weary summer day.
CAN: I can't tell whether deceiving spirits hover over this place, or whether it is the warm, heavenly imagination in my own heart that turns everything around me into paradise. Just in front of the house there is a spring — a spring that holds me under a spell, like Melusina and her sisters. You go down a gentle slope and come to an archway, and some twenty steps further down the clearest crystal water gushes from the marble rock. The low wall that encloses it at the top, the tall trees that ring the spot, the coolness of the place itself — it all makes an impression at once pleasant and solemn. Not a day goes by that I don't spend an hour there. The young girls come out from town to fetch water — innocent, necessary work that once even kings' daughters did. When I sit and rest there, the old patriarchal life comes alive around me: I can see them, our forefathers, striking up friendships and arranging matches at the well, and I feel how kindly spirits watched over the springs and streams. Anyone who can't share these feelings has never really known the relief of resting by cool water after a hard summer's day.

=== Ch 5: May 13, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: You ask if you shall send me books. My dear friend, I beseech you, for the love of God, relieve me from such a yoke! I need no more to be guided, agitated, heated. My heart ferments sufficiently of itself. I want strains to lull me, and I find them to perfection in my Homer. Often do I strive to allay the burning fever of my blood; and you have never witnessed anything so unsteady, so uncertain, as my heart. But need I confess this to you, my dear friend, who have so often endured the anguish of witnessing my sudden transitions from sorrow to immoderate joy, and from sweet melancholy to violent passions? I treat my poor heart like a sick child, and gratify its every fancy. Do not mention this again: there are people who would censure me for it.
CAN: You ask whether you should send me books. Dear friend, for the love of God, spare me that yoke! I don't want to be guided, stirred up, inflamed any more — my heart seethes quite enough on its own. What I need is something to lull me, and I find it perfectly in my Homer. How often I try to quiet the fever in my blood; you have never seen anything so changeable, so unsteady, as this heart of mine. But I hardly need confess that to you, who have so often had to watch me lurch from grief to wild joy, from sweet melancholy to violent passion. I treat my poor heart like a sick child and let it have whatever it wants. Don't repeat this — there are people who would hold it against me.

=== Ch 6: May 15, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: The common people of the place know me already, and love me, particularly the children. When at first I associated with them, and inquired in a friendly tone about their various trifles, some fancied that I wished to ridicule them, and turned from me in exceeding ill-humour. I did not allow that circumstance to grieve me: I only felt most keenly what I have often before observed. Persons who can claim a certain rank keep themselves coldly aloof from the common people, as though they feared to lose their importance by the contact; whilst wanton idlers, and such as are prone to bad joking, affect to descend to their level, only to make the poor people feel their impertinence all the more keenly.
CAN: The common people here already know me and are fond of me — the children especially. When I first spent time with them and asked in a friendly way about their little affairs, some imagined I was mocking them and turned away in real ill humor. I didn't let it grieve me; I only felt, more sharply than ever, something I have often noticed before. People who can lay claim to a certain rank keep coldly aloof from the common folk, as if they feared losing their dignity by the contact; while frivolous idlers and the sort who like a cruel joke pretend to come down to their level, only to make the poor feel their insolence the more.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: I know very well that we are not all equal, nor can be so; but it is my opinion that he who avoids the common people, in order not to lose their respect, is as much to blame as a coward who hides himself from his enemy because he fears defeat.
CAN: I know perfectly well that we are not all equal, and cannot be. But it is my view that a man who keeps his distance from the common people in order not to lose their respect is as much to blame as a coward who hides from his enemy for fear of losing.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: The other day I went to the fountain, and found a young servant-girl, who had set her pitcher on the lowest step, and looked around to see if one of her companions was approaching to place it on her head. I ran down, and looked at her. "Shall I help you, pretty lass?" said I. She blushed deeply. "Oh, sir!" she exclaimed. "No ceremony!" I replied. She adjusted her head-gear, and I helped her. She thanked me, and ascended the steps.
CAN: The other day I went down to the spring and found a young servant girl who had set her pitcher on the bottom step and was looking round to see whether one of her friends was coming to help lift it onto her head. I went down and looked at her. "Shall I help you, my dear?" I said. She turned bright red. "Oh no, sir!" she said. "Don't stand on ceremony," I replied. She straightened the pad on her head, and I helped her. She thanked me and climbed the steps.

=== Ch 7: May 17, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I have made all sorts of acquaintances, but have as yet found no society. I know not what attraction I possess for the people, so many of them like me, and attach themselves to me; and then I feel sorry when the road we pursue together goes only a short distance. If you inquire what the people are like here, I must answer, "The same as everywhere." The human race is but a monotonous affair. Most of them labour the greater part of their time for mere subsistence; and the scanty portion of freedom which remains to them so troubles them that they use every exertion to get rid of it. Oh, the destiny of man!
CAN: I have struck up all kinds of acquaintances, but found no real companionship yet. I don't know what it is about me that draws people; so many of them take to me and attach themselves to me, and it pains me when our paths only run together a little way. If you ask what the people here are like, I have to say: the same as everywhere. The human race is a monotonous business. Most of them spend the better part of their lives working just to stay alive, and the scrap of freedom that is left over frightens them so much that they will do anything to be rid of it. Oh, the lot of man!
--- para 1 ---
SRC: But they are a right good sort of people. If I occasionally forget myself, and take part in the innocent pleasures which are not yet forbidden to the peasantry, and enjoy myself, for instance, with genuine freedom and sincerity, round a well-covered table, or arrange an excursion or a dance opportunely, and so forth, all this produces a good effect upon my disposition; only I must forget that there lie dormant within me so many other qualities which moulder uselessly, and which I am obliged to keep carefully concealed. Ah! this thought affects my spirits fearfully. And yet to be misunderstood is the fate of the like of us.
CAN: But they are a thoroughly good sort. When I let myself go now and then and share the simple pleasures still left to ordinary people — sitting down, say, with real openness and good cheer at a well-laid table, or getting up a drive or a dance at the right moment, and so on — it does me a world of good; only I mustn't remember that so many other powers lie idle in me, rotting unused, that I have to keep carefully hidden. Ah, that thought wrings my heart. And yet to be misunderstood is the fate of people like us.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: Alas, that the friend of my youth is gone! Alas, that I ever knew her! I might say to myself, "You are a dreamer to seek what is not to be found here below." But she has been mine. I have possessed that heart, that noble soul, in whose presence I seemed to be more than I really was, because I was all that I could be. Good heavens! did then a single power of my soul remain unexercised? In her presence could I not display, to its full extent, that mysterious feeling with which my heart embraces nature? Was not our intercourse a perpetual web of the finest emotions, of the keenest wit, the varieties of which, even in their very eccentricity, bore the stamp of genius? Alas! the few years by which she was my senior brought her to the grave before me. Never can I forget her firm mind or her heavenly patience.
CAN: How sad that the friend of my youth is gone; how sad that I ever knew her! I could tell myself: you are a fool to look for what can't be found here on earth. But she was mine. I held that heart, that noble soul, and in her presence I seemed to be more than I was, because I was everything I could be. Good God — was a single power of my soul left unused? Couldn't I unfold, in front of her, the whole of that mysterious feeling with which my heart takes in nature? Wasn't our being together an endless weave of the finest feeling and the sharpest wit, whose every variation, even at its most playful, bore the mark of genius? And now — the few years she had over me carried her to the grave before me. I will never forget her steady mind, her heavenly patience.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: A few days ago I met a certain young V—, a frank, open fellow, with a most pleasing countenance. He has just left the university, does not deem himself overwise, but believes he knows more than other people. He has worked hard, as I can perceive from many circumstances, and, in short, possesses a large stock of information. When he heard that I am drawing a good deal, and that I know Greek (two wonderful things for this part of the country), he came to see me, and displayed his whole store of learning, from Batteaux to Wood, from De Piles to Winkelmann: he assured me he had read through the first part of Sultzer's theory, and also possessed a manuscript of Heyne's work on the study of the antique. I allowed it all to pass.
CAN: A few days ago I met a young man named V—, a frank, open fellow with a very pleasant face. He has just come from the university; he doesn't think himself especially wise, but he does believe he knows more than other people. He has worked hard, as I can tell in all sorts of ways — in short, he is well informed. When he heard that I draw a good deal and that I know Greek (two rare things in this part of the country), he sought me out and unloaded his whole stock of learning, from Batteux to Wood, from De Piles to Winckelmann; he assured me he had read all the way through the first part of Sulzer's theory and owned a manuscript of Heyne's work on the study of antiquity. I let it all pass.
--- para 4 ---
SRC: I have become acquainted, also, with a very worthy person, the district judge, a frank and open-hearted man. I am told it is a most delightful thing to see him in the midst of his children, of whom he has nine. His eldest daughter especially is highly spoken of. He has invited me to go and see him, and I intend to do so on the first opportunity. He lives at one of the royal hunting-lodges, which can be reached from here in an hour and a half by walking, and which he obtained leave to inhabit after the loss of his wife, as it is so painful to him to reside in town and at the court.
CAN: I have also got to know a very decent man, the district steward — a frank, warm-hearted soul. They tell me it is a real joy to see him among his children; he has nine, and his eldest daughter especially is much spoken of. He has invited me to visit, and I mean to go at the first chance. He lives at one of the prince's hunting lodges, an hour and a half's walk from here, which he was given leave to move into after his wife's death, since living in town and at the court was too painful for him.
--- para 5 ---
SRC: There have also come in my way a few other originals of a questionable sort, who are in all respects undesirable, and most intolerable in their demonstration of friendship. Good-bye. This letter will please you: it is quite historical.
CAN: A few other oddities of a dubious kind have also crossed my path — undesirable in every way, and most unbearable in their shows of friendship. Goodbye. You will like this letter: it is quite a chronicle.

=== Ch 8: May 22, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: That the life of man is but a dream, many a man has surmised heretofore; and I, too, am everywhere pursued by this feeling. When I consider the narrow limits within which our active and inquiring faculties are confined; when I see how all our energies are wasted in providing for mere necessities, which again have no further end than to prolong a wretched existence; and then that all our satisfaction concerning certain subjects of investigation ends in nothing better than a passive resignation, whilst we amuse ourselves painting our prison-walls with bright figures and brilliant landscapes,—when I consider all this, Wilhelm, I am silent. I examine my own being, and find there a world, but a world rather of imagination and dim desires, than of distinctness and living power. Then everything swims before my senses, and I smile and dream while pursuing my way through the world.
CAN: That a man's life is only a dream — many have felt this before, and the feeling follows me everywhere too. When I see the narrow limits that pen in our active, searching powers; when I see how all our efforts go to providing for mere needs that themselves have no purpose beyond stretching out a poor existence; and how all our reassurance on certain questions we probe is just a dreamy resignation, while we paint the walls of our prison with bright figures and shining views — all this, Wilhelm, leaves me silent. I turn back into myself and find a world there — but more a world of intimation and dim longing than of clear, living force. And then everything blurs before my senses, and I go on through the world smiling and dreaming.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: All learned professors and doctors are agreed that children do not comprehend the cause of their desires; but that the grown-up should wander about this earth like children, without knowing whence they come, or whither they go, influenced as little by fixed motives, but guided like them by biscuits, sugar-plums, and the rod,—this is what nobody is willing to acknowledge; and yet I think it is palpable.
CAN: Every learned schoolmaster and tutor agrees that children don't know why they want what they want; but that grown men too should stagger about this earth like children, not knowing where they come from or where they are going, steered just as little by real purposes, led instead by biscuits and cakes and the birch — nobody likes to admit that, though it seems plain enough to me.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: I know what you will say in reply; for I am ready to admit that they are happiest, who, like children, amuse themselves with their playthings, dress and undress their dolls, and attentively watch the cupboard, where mamma has locked up her sweet things, and, when at last they get a delicious morsel, eat it greedily, and exclaim, "More!" These are certainly happy beings; but others also are objects of envy, who dignify their paltry employments, and sometimes even their passions, with pompous titles, representing them to mankind as gigantic achievements performed for their welfare and glory. But the man who humbly acknowledges the vanity of all this, who observes with what pleasure the thriving citizen converts his little garden into a paradise, and how patiently even the poor man pursues his weary way under his burden, and how all wish equally to behold the light of the sun a little longer,—yes, such a man is at peace, and creates his own world within himself; and he is also happy, because he is a man. And then, however limited his sphere, he still preserves in his bosom the sweet feeling of liberty, and knows that he can quit his prison whenever he likes.
CAN: I know what you will say to this, and I am ready to grant that the happiest people are the ones who live for the day like children, who drag their dolls about and dress and undress them, who hang around the drawer where mother has locked up the sweets, and when they finally get their treat, gobble it down and cry "More!" Those are happy creatures. And there is another kind to be envied too — those who give their trifling occupations, even their passions, grand titles, and present them to the world as towering deeds done for its welfare and glory. Happy the man who can be like that! But anyone who humbly recognizes what all of this comes to; who sees how neatly the contented citizen trims his little garden into a paradise, how patiently even the poor man carries his load along his weary road, and how all of them want, equally, just to see the sunlight a little longer — he stays calm, and shapes his own world out of himself, and is happy too, because he is a human being. And then, hemmed in as he is, he still keeps in his heart that sweet feeling of freedom, and the knowledge that he can leave this prison whenever he chooses.

=== Ch 9: May 26, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: You know of old my ways of settling anywhere, of selecting a little cottage in some cosy spot, and of putting up in it with every inconvenience. Here, too, I have discovered such a snug, comfortable place, which possesses peculiar charms for me.
CAN: You know my old habit of settling in wherever I go — picking out a little cottage in some snug corner and putting up with every discomfort. Here too I have found just such a cosy, comfortable spot, one with its own peculiar charm for me.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: About a league from the town is a place called Walheim. (The reader need not take the trouble to look for the place thus designated. We have found it necessary to change the names given in the original.) It is delightfully situated on the side of a hill; and, by proceeding along one of the footpaths which lead out of the village, you can have a view of the whole valley. A good old woman lives there, who keeps a small inn. She sells wine, beer, and coffee, and is cheerful and pleasant notwithstanding her age. The chief charm of this spot consists in two linden-trees, spreading their enormous branches over the little green before the church, which is entirely surrounded by peasants' cottages, barns, and homesteads. I have seldom seen a place so retired and peaceable; and there often have my table and chair brought out from the little inn, and drink my coffee there, and read my Homer. Accident brought me to the spot one fine afternoon, and I found it perfectly deserted. Everybody was in the fields except a little boy about four years of age, who was sitting on the ground, and held between his knees a child about six months old: he pressed it to his bosom with both arms, which thus formed a sort of arm-chair; and, notwithstanding the liveliness which sparkled in its black eyes, it remained perfectly still. The sight charmed me. I sat down upon a plough opposite, and sketched with great delight this little picture of brotherly tenderness. I added the neighbouring hedge, the barn-door, and some broken cart-wheels, just as they happened to lie; and I found in about an hour that I had made a very correct and interesting drawing, without putting in the slightest thing of my own. This confirmed me in my resolution of adhering, for the future, entirely to nature. She alone is inexhaustible, and capable of forming the greatest masters. Much may be alleged in favour of rules, as much may be likewise advanced in favour of the laws of society: an artist formed upon them will never produce anything absolutely bad or disgusting; as a man who observes the laws, and obeys decorum, can never be an absolutely intolerable neighbour, nor a decided villain: but yet, say what you will of rules, they destroy the genuine feeling of nature, as well as its true expression. Do not tell me "that this is too hard, that they only restrain and prune superfluous branches, etc." My good friend, I will illustrate this by an analogy. These things resemble love. A warmhearted youth becomes strongly attached to a maiden: he spends every hour of the day in her company, wears out his health, and lavishes his fortune, to afford continual proof that he is wholly devoted to her. Then comes a man of the world, a man of place and respectability, and addresses him thus: "My good young friend, love is natural; but you must love within bounds. Divide your time: devote a portion to business, and give the hours of recreation to your mistress. Calculate your fortune; and out of the superfluity you may make her a present, only not too often,—on her birthday, and such occasions." Pursuing this advice, he may become a useful member of society, and I should advise every prince to give him an appointment; but it is all up with his love, and with his genius if he be an artist. O my friend! why is it that the torrent of genius so seldom bursts forth, so seldom rolls in full-flowing stream, overwhelming your astounded soul? Because, on either side of this stream, cold and respectable persons have taken up their abodes, and, forsooth, their summer-houses and tulip-beds would suffer from the torrent; wherefore they dig trenches, and raise embankments betimes, in order to avert the impending danger.
CAN: About a league from the town there is a place called Walheim. (The reader needn't bother looking for the place under that name; we have had to change the real names given in the original.) It sits beautifully on the side of a hill, and if you follow one of the footpaths out of the village, you get a view of the whole valley. A good old woman lives there and keeps a little inn; she sells wine, beer, and coffee, and for all her age she is cheerful and pleasant. The real charm of the place is two linden trees that spread their huge branches over the small green in front of the church, which is ringed all around by peasants' cottages, barns, and farmyards. I have rarely seen anywhere so quiet and out of the way, and I often have my table and chair brought out from the inn and drink my coffee there and read my Homer. I happened on the spot one fine afternoon and found it completely deserted; everyone was out in the fields except a little boy of about four, who was sitting on the ground holding a baby of about six months between his knees, pressing it to his chest with both arms so that they made a kind of armchair. For all the liveliness sparkling in its dark eyes, the baby sat perfectly still. The sight charmed me. I sat down on a plough opposite and sketched this little picture of brotherly tenderness with real delight. I added the hedge nearby, the barn door, and a few broken cartwheels, just as they lay; and after about an hour I found I had made a well-composed, interesting drawing without putting in anything of my own. That confirmed me in my resolve to keep, from now on, entirely to nature. Nature alone is inexhaustible, and nature alone forms great masters. You can say a great deal in favor of rules — much the same as can be said for the conventions of society: a man trained on them will never produce anything downright bad or offensive, just as a man who keeps the law and minds his manners can never be an utterly unbearable neighbor or an outright villain. But say what you like, rules destroy the true feeling of nature and the true expression of it. You'll tell me that's too severe — that rules only restrain, only prune away rank growth, and so on. My good friend, let me give you an analogy. It is just like love. A warm-hearted young man gives himself wholly to a girl, spends every hour of the day with her, wears out his health and pours out his money to prove again and again that he is utterly devoted to her. Then along comes some man of the world, some man of office and standing, and says to him: "My dear young fellow, love is natural — but you must love within limits. Divide up your time: give part of it to your work and save your free hours for your sweetheart. Reckon up your money, and from whatever is left over you may make her a present — only not too often: on her birthday, say, and the like." If the young man takes this advice, he'll make a useful member of society, and I'd urge any prince to give him a post — but it's all over with his love, and, if he's an artist, with his art. Oh, my friend! Why is it that the torrent of genius so seldom breaks loose, so seldom comes roaring down in a full flood to shake your astonished soul? Because along both banks of the stream live cool, respectable people whose summer-houses and tulip beds would be ruined by it, and who therefore dig their channels and throw up their dikes in good time to head off the danger.

=== Ch 10: May 27, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I find I have fallen into raptures, declamation, and similes, and have forgotten, in consequence, to tell you what became of the children. Absorbed in my artistic contemplations, which I briefly described in my letter of yesterday, I continued sitting on the plough for two hours. Toward evening a young woman, with a basket on her arm, came running toward the children, who had not moved all that time. She exclaimed from a distance, "You are a good boy, Philip!" She gave me greeting: I returned it, rose, and approached her. I inquired if she were the mother of those pretty children. "Yes," she said; and, giving the eldest a piece of bread, she took the little one in her arms and kissed it with a mother's tenderness. "I left my child in Philip's care," she said, "whilst I went into the town with my eldest boy to buy some wheaten bread, some sugar, and an earthen pot." I saw the various articles in the basket, from which the cover had fallen. "I shall make some broth to-night for my little Hans (which was the name of the youngest): that wild fellow, the big one, broke my pot yesterday, whilst he was scrambling with Philip for what remained of the contents." I inquired for the eldest; and she had scarcely time to tell me that he was driving a couple of geese home from the meadow, when he ran up, and handed Philip an osier-twig. I talked a little longer with the woman, and found that she was the daughter of the schoolmaster, and that her husband was gone on a journey into Switzerland for some money a relation had left him. "They wanted to cheat him," she said, "and would not answer his letters; so he is gone there himself. I hope he has met with no accident, as I have heard nothing of him since his departure." I left the woman, with regret, giving each of the children a kreutzer, with an additional one for the youngest, to buy some wheaten bread for his broth when she went to town next; and so we parted. I assure you, my dear friend, when my thoughts are all in tumult, the sight of such a creature as this tranquillises my disturbed mind. She moves in a happy thoughtlessness within the confined circle of her existence; she supplies her wants from day to day; and, when she sees the leaves fall, they raise no other idea in her mind than that winter is approaching. Since that time I have gone out there frequently. The children have become quite familiar with me; and each gets a lump of sugar when I drink my coffee, and they share my milk and bread and butter in the evening. They always receive their kreutzer on Sundays, for the good woman has orders to give it to them when I do not go there after evening service. They are quite at home with me, tell me everything; and I am particularly amused with observing their tempers, and the simplicity of their behaviour, when some of the other village children are assembled with them.
CAN: I see I have fallen into raptures and grand talk and similes, and so forgotten to tell you what became of the children. Wrapped up in my artistic musings, which I sketched out for you yesterday, I went on sitting on that plough for a good two hours. Toward evening a young woman came hurrying toward the children, who hadn't stirred the whole time, with a basket on her arm, calling out from a distance, "Philip, you've been such a good boy!" She greeted me; I returned it, got up, and went over. I asked whether she was the mother of these pretty children. "Yes," she said, and giving the older one a piece of bread, she picked up the baby and kissed it with all a mother's tenderness. "I left my little one in Philip's care," she said, "while I went into town with my eldest to buy some white bread and sugar and an earthenware pot." I could see all of it in the basket, the lid having slipped off. "I want to make some broth tonight for my little Hans" — the baby — "but that wild one, the eldest, smashed my pot yesterday squabbling with Philip over the scrapings." I asked after the eldest, and she had barely told me he was driving a couple of geese home from the meadow when he came running up and handed Philip a willow switch. I talked with the woman a while longer and learned that she was the schoolmaster's daughter, and that her husband had gone off to Switzerland to collect a legacy left him by a relative. "They tried to cheat him out of it," she said, "and wouldn't answer his letters, so he's gone there himself. I only hope nothing has happened to him; I've heard nothing since he left." I found it hard to leave the woman; I gave each of the children a kreutzer, and one more for the baby, so she could bring it some white bread for its broth next time she went to town, and we parted. I tell you, dear friend, when my thoughts are all in an uproar, the sight of a creature like this settles my troubled mind. She moves contentedly, without a care, around the small circle of her life, getting through one day at a time; and when she sees the leaves fall it means nothing to her but that winter is coming. Since then I've gone out there often. The children have grown quite used to me; they get sugar when I drink my coffee, and in the evening they share my bread and butter and milk. On Sundays they always get their kreutzer, for the good woman has orders to hand it to them when I'm not there after the evening service. They're entirely at ease with me and tell me everything, and I get special pleasure from watching their little tempers and the simplicity of their ways when a few of the other village children are gathered with them.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: It has given me a deal of trouble to satisfy the anxiety of the mother, lest (as she says) "they should inconvenience the gentleman."
CAN: It cost me no end of trouble to set the mother's mind at rest, since she worried that they might "be a nuisance to the gentleman."

=== Ch 11: May 30, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: What I have lately said of painting is equally true with respect to poetry. It is only necessary for us to know what is really excellent, and venture to give it expression; and that is saying much in few words. To-day I have had a scene, which, if literally related, would, make the most beautiful idyl in the world. But why should I talk of poetry and scenes and idyls? Can we never take pleasure in nature without having recourse to art?
CAN: What I said the other day about painting holds just as true for poetry. The whole thing is simply to recognize what is truly excellent and dare to put it into words — and that says a great deal in a few. Today I had a scene that, told plainly, would make the loveliest idyll in the world. But why talk of poetry and scenes and idylls? Can we never just take pleasure in nature without dressing it up as art?
--- para 1 ---
SRC: If you expect anything grand or magnificent from this introduction, you will be sadly mistaken. It relates merely to a peasant-lad, who has excited in me the warmest interest. As usual, I shall tell my story badly; and you, as usual, will think me extravagant. It is Walheim once more—always Walheim—which produces these wonderful phenomena.
CAN: If after that opening you're expecting something grand or magnificent, you'll be badly let down. It's nothing more than a peasant lad who has stirred the warmest interest in me. As usual I'll tell it badly, and as usual you'll think I'm overdoing it. It's Walheim again — always Walheim — that produces these wonders.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: A party had assembled outside the house under the linden-trees, to drink coffee. The company did not exactly please me; and, under one pretext or another, I lingered behind.
CAN: A party had gathered outside the inn under the lindens to drink coffee. The company didn't quite suit me, so on one pretext or another I hung back.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: A peasant came from an adjoining house, and set to work arranging some part of the same plough which I had lately sketched. His appearance pleased me; and I spoke to him, inquired about his circumstances, made his acquaintance, and, as is my wont with persons of that class, was soon admitted into his confidence. He said he was in the service of a young widow, who set great store by him. He spoke so much of his mistress, and praised her so extravagantly, that I could soon see he was desperately in love with her. "She is no longer young," he said: "and she was treated so badly by her former husband that she does not mean to marry again." From his account it was so evident what incomparable charms she possessed for him, and how ardently he wished she would select him to extinguish the recollection of her first husband's misconduct, that I should have to repeat his own words in order to describe the depth of the poor fellow's attachment, truth, and devotion. It would, in fact, require the gifts of a great poet to convey the expression of his features, the harmony of his voice, and the heavenly fire of his eye. No words can portray the tenderness of his every movement and of every feature: no effort of mine could do justice to the scene. His alarm lest I should misconceive his position with regard to his mistress, or question the propriety of her conduct, touched me particularly. The charming manner with which he described her form and person, which, without possessing the graces of youth, won and attached him to her, is inexpressible, and must be left to the imagination. I have never in my life witnessed or fancied or conceived the possibility of such intense devotion, such ardent affections, united with so much purity. Do not blame me if I say that the recollection of this innocence and truth is deeply impressed upon my very soul; that this picture of fidelity and tenderness haunts me everywhere; and that my own heart, as though enkindled by the flame, glows and burns within me.
CAN: A peasant came out of a nearby house and set about repairing part of the same plough I'd sketched a little while ago. I liked the look of him, so I spoke to him, asked about his situation, and we were soon acquainted — and, as usually happens with people of that class, he was soon confiding in me. He told me he was working for a young widow who thought very highly of him. He talked so much about his mistress and praised her so extravagantly that I could see at once he was head over heels in love with her. "She's no longer young," he said, "and her first husband treated her so badly that she has no intention of marrying again." From everything he said it was so plain what matchless charms she had for him, and how desperately he wished she would choose him to wipe out the memory of her first husband's cruelty, that I'd have to repeat his very words to give you any idea of the poor fellow's devotion, sincerity, and longing. I'd need a great poet's gifts to convey the play of his features, the music of his voice, the secret fire in his eyes. No words can capture the tenderness in his every movement and look; anything I might say would fall short. What touched me most was his anxiety that I might think badly of how things stood between him and his mistress, or doubt her good conduct. The charming way he described her figure and her person — which, though without the graces of youth, had drawn him to her and bound him fast — I can't reproduce; you'll have to imagine it. In all my life I've never seen, never pictured, never even thought possible, such intense devotion, such ardent longing, joined with so much purity. Don't blame me when I say that the memory of this innocence and truth has sunk deep into my soul, that this image of faithfulness and tenderness follows me everywhere, and that my own heart, as if kindled by the same flame, glows and burns within me.
--- para 4 ---
SRC: I mean now to try and see her as soon as I can: or perhaps, on second thoughts, I had better not; it is better I should behold her through the eyes of her lover. To my sight, perhaps, she would not appear as she now stands before me; and why should I destroy so sweet a picture?
CAN: I mean to go and see her as soon as I can — or rather, on second thought, perhaps I'd better not. Better to keep seeing her through her lover's eyes; to my own she might not look the way she stands before me now, and why spoil so sweet a picture?

=== Ch 12: June 16, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: "Why do I not write to you?" You lay claim to learning, and ask such a question. You should have guessed that I am well—that is to say—in a word, I have made an acquaintance who has won my heart: I have—I know not.
CAN: "Why don't I write to you?" And you call yourself a learned man, asking that. You ought to have guessed that I'm well — in fact — in a word, I've met someone who has taken hold of my heart. I have — I hardly know.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: To give you a regular account of the manner in which I have become acquainted with the most amiable of women would be a difficult task. I am a happy and contented mortal, but a poor historian.
CAN: To give you a proper, orderly account of how I came to know the most lovely of women would be beyond me. I'm happy and content, and a poor chronicler for it.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: An angel! Nonsense! Everybody so describes his mistress; and yet I find it impossible to tell you how perfect she is, or why she is so perfect: suffice it to say she has captivated all my senses.
CAN: An angel! Nonsense — every man calls his sweetheart that. And yet I can't manage to tell you how perfect she is, or why she's so perfect; enough to say she has captured every one of my senses.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: So much simplicity with so much understanding—so mild, and yet so resolute—a mind so placid, and a life so active.
CAN: So much simplicity together with so much intelligence; so gentle, and yet so firm; a soul so serene, and a life so full of activity.
--- para 4 ---
SRC: But all this is ugly balderdash, which expresses not a single character nor feature. Some other time—but no, not some other time, now, this very instant, will I tell you all about it. Now or never. Well, between ourselves, since I commenced my letter, I have been three times on the point of throwing down my pen, of ordering my horse, and riding out. And yet I vowed this morning that I would not ride to-day, and yet every moment I am rushing to the window to see how high the sun is.
CAN: But all this is clumsy nonsense — abstractions that catch not a single trait of her, not one feature. Another time — no, not another time, now, this very instant, I'll tell you everything. Now or never. Well, just between us: since I started this letter I've three times been on the point of throwing down my pen, calling for my horse, and riding out — even though I swore this morning I wouldn't ride today, and even though I keep running to the window every other minute to see how high the sun has climbed.
--- para 5 ---
SRC: I could not restrain myself—go to her I must. I have just returned, Wilhelm; and whilst I am taking supper I will write to you. What a delight it was for my soul to see her in the midst of her dear, beautiful children,—eight brothers and sisters!
CAN: I couldn't hold out — go to her I had to. Now I'm back, Wilhelm, and I'll write to you over my supper. What joy it was for my soul to see her surrounded by her dear, beautiful little brothers and sisters — eight of them!
--- para 6 ---
SRC: But, if I proceed thus, you will be no wiser at the end of my letter than you were at the beginning. Attend, then, and I will compel myself to give you the details.
CAN: But if I go on like this you'll be none the wiser at the end of the letter than at the start. So listen: I'll make myself give you the particulars.
--- para 7 ---
SRC: I mentioned to you the other day that I had become acquainted with S—, the district judge, and that he had invited me to go and visit him in his retirement, or rather in his little kingdom. But I neglected going, and perhaps should never have gone, if chance had not discovered to me the treasure which lay concealed in that retired spot. Some of our young people had proposed giving a ball in the country, at which I consented to be present. I offered my hand for the evening to a pretty and agreeable, but rather commonplace, sort of girl from the immediate neighbourhood; and it was agreed that I should engage a carriage, and call upon Charlotte, with my partner and her aunt, to convey them to the ball. My companion informed me, as we drove along through the park to the hunting-lodge, that I should make the acquaintance of a very charming young lady. "Take care," added the aunt, "that you do not lose your heart." "Why?" said I. "Because she is already engaged to a very worthy man," she replied, "who is gone to settle his affairs upon the death of his father, and will succeed to a very considerable inheritance." This information possessed no interest for me. When we arrived at the gate, the sun was setting behind the tops of the mountains. The atmosphere was heavy; and the ladies expressed their fears of an approaching storm, as masses of low black clouds were gathering in the horizon. I relieved their anxieties by pretending to be weather-wise, although I myself had some apprehensions lest our pleasure should be interrupted.
CAN: I mentioned the other day that I'd got to know S—, the district steward, and that he'd invited me out to see him in his retreat — his little kingdom, rather. I put it off, and might never have gone if chance hadn't shown me the treasure hidden away in that quiet spot. Some of our young people had got up a country ball, and I agreed to go. I offered to escort a pretty, pleasant, but rather ordinary girl from the neighborhood, and it was settled that I'd hire a carriage and drive out with my partner and her aunt to collect Charlotte on the way. "You'll meet a very lovely young woman," my companion told me as we drove through the woods toward the hunting lodge. "Take care you don't lose your heart," the aunt added. "Why?" I asked. "Because she's already promised to a very fine man," she said, "who's away just now settling his affairs after his father's death, and stands to come into a sizable inheritance." The news meant nothing to me. As we reached the gate the sun was just going down behind the mountaintops. The air was heavy, and the ladies fretted about a storm coming on, with banks of low black cloud massing on the horizon. I soothed them by playing the weather prophet, though I had my own misgivings that our evening might be spoiled.
--- para 8 ---
SRC: I alighted; and a maid came to the door, and requested us to wait a moment for her mistress. I walked across the court to a well-built house, and, ascending the flight of steps in front, opened the door, and saw before me the most charming spectacle I had ever witnessed. Six children, from eleven to two years old, were running about the hall, and surrounding a lady of middle height, with a lovely figure, dressed in a robe of simple white, trimmed with pink ribbons. She was holding a rye loaf in her hand, and was cutting slices for the little ones all around, in proportion to their age and appetite. She performed her task in a graceful and affectionate manner; each claimant awaiting his turn with outstretched hands, and boisterously shouting his thanks. Some of them ran away at once, to enjoy their evening meal; whilst others, of a gentler disposition, retired to the courtyard to see the strangers, and to survey the carriage in which their Charlotte was to drive away. "Pray forgive me for giving you the trouble to come for me, and for keeping the ladies waiting: but dressing, and arranging some household duties before I leave, had made me forget my children's supper; and they do not like to take it from any one but me." I uttered some indifferent compliment: but my whole soul was absorbed by her air, her voice, her manner; and I had scarcely recovered myself when she ran into her room to fetch her gloves and fan. The young ones threw inquiring glances at me from a distance; whilst I approached the youngest, a most delicious little creature. He drew back; and Charlotte, entering at the very moment, said, "Louis, shake hands with your cousin." The little fellow obeyed willingly; and I could not resist giving him a hearty kiss, notwithstanding his rather dirty face. "Cousin," said I to Charlotte, as I handed her down, "do you think I deserve the happiness of being related to you?" She replied, with a ready smile, "Oh! I have such a number of cousins, that I should be sorry if you were the most undeserving of them." In taking leave, she desired her next sister, Sophy, a girl about eleven years old, to take great care of the children, and to say good-bye to papa for her when he came home from his ride. She enjoined to the little ones to obey their sister Sophy as they would herself, upon which some promised that they would; but a little fair-haired girl, about six years old, looked discontented, and said, "But Sophy is not you, Charlotte; and we like you best." The two eldest boys had clambered up the carriage; and, at my request, she permitted them to accompany us a little way through the forest, upon their promising to sit very still, and hold fast.
CAN: I got down, and a maid came to the door and asked us to wait a moment for her mistress. I crossed the courtyard to the handsome house, went up the front steps, and as I opened the door I saw the most charming sight I have ever laid eyes on. Six children, from eleven years old down to two, were rushing about the hall around a girl of middle height with a lovely figure, in a plain white dress trimmed with pink ribbons. She held a loaf of dark bread and was cutting slices for the little ones around her, each according to its age and appetite, handing them out with such grace and affection, every child waiting its turn with hands outstretched and shouting out its thanks. Some ran off at once with their supper, while the gentler ones walked out to the gate to look at the strangers and the carriage that was to carry their Charlotte away. "Do forgive me for making you come in for me, and for keeping the ladies waiting," she said. "What with dressing and arranging a few household things before I go out, I forgot to give the children their supper, and they won't take it from anyone but me." I made some empty compliment, but my whole soul was lost in her bearing, her voice, her way of moving, and I had barely collected myself when she darted into her room for her gloves and fan. The children eyed me from a little way off, and I went over to the youngest, the most delightful little creature. He drew back, and just then Charlotte came in and said, "Louis, give your cousin your hand." The little fellow did so readily, and I couldn't help giving him a hearty kiss in spite of his runny nose. "Cousin?" I said to Charlotte as I handed her down. "Do you think I deserve the good fortune of being related to you?" "Oh," she answered with an easy smile, "I have such a crowd of cousins that I'd be sorry to think you the least deserving of them." As she left she asked her next sister, Sophy, a girl of about eleven, to keep a close eye on the children and to say goodbye to their father for her when he got back from his ride. She told the little ones to mind their sister Sophy as if she were Charlotte herself, and some of them promised they would; but one fair-haired little girl of about six looked unhappy and said, "But Sophy isn't you, Charlotte, and we like you best." The two eldest boys had scrambled up onto the carriage, and at my request she let them ride a little way with us through the wood, on their promise to sit quite still and hold on tight.
--- para 9 ---
SRC: We were hardly seated, and the ladies had scarcely exchanged compliments, making the usual remarks upon each other's dress, and upon the company they expected to meet, when Charlotte stopped the carriage, and made her brothers get down. They insisted upon kissing her hands once more; which the eldest did with all the tenderness of a youth of fifteen, but the other in a lighter and more careless manner. She desired them again to give her love to the children, and we drove off.
CAN: We had barely settled in and the ladies barely finished their courtesies — the usual remarks on each other's dress and on the company we'd find — when Charlotte stopped the carriage and had her brothers climb down. They insisted on kissing her hand once more, the elder with all the tenderness of a boy of fifteen, the other more lightly and carelessly. She sent her love again to the children, and we drove on.
--- para 10 ---
SRC: The aunt inquired of Charlotte whether she had finished the book she had last sent her. "No," said Charlotte; "I did not like it: you can have it again. And the one before was not much better." I was surprised, upon asking the title, to hear that it was ____. (We feel obliged to suppress the passage in the letter, to prevent any one from feeling aggrieved; although no author need pay much attention to the opinion of a mere girl, or that of an unsteady young man.)

I was surprised, when I asked the title, to hear it was ——. --- para 11 ---
SRC: I found penetration and character in everything she said: every expression seemed to brighten her features with new charms,—with new rays of genius,—which unfolded by degrees, as she felt herself understood.
CAN: Everything she said showed insight and character; with every remark her face seemed to light up with fresh charm, fresh sparks of spirit, that unfolded little by little as she felt herself understood.
--- para 12 ---
SRC: "When I was younger," she observed, "I loved nothing so much as romances. Nothing could equal my delight when, on some holiday, I could settle down quietly in a corner, and enter with my whole heart and soul into the joys or sorrows of some fictitious Leonora. I do not deny that they even possess some charms for me yet. But I read so seldom, that I prefer books suited exactly to my taste. And I like those authors best whose scenes describe my own situation in life,—and the friends who are about me, whose stories touch me with interest, from resembling my own homely existence,—which, without being absolutely paradise, is, on the whole, a source of indescribable happiness."
CAN: "When I was younger," she said, "I loved nothing so much as novels. You can't imagine my delight when I could settle into a corner on some quiet holiday and throw myself heart and soul into the joys and sorrows of some made-up Leonora. I won't pretend that kind of book has lost all its charm for me even now. But since I read so seldom, I want books exactly to my taste. And I like best the authors whose world is my own — books about the life around me, about the people I know, whose stories grip me because they're so like my own plain existence, which, without being paradise, is on the whole a source of happiness I can't put into words."
--- para 13 ---
SRC: I endeavoured to conceal the emotion which these words occasioned, but it was of slight avail; for, when she had expressed so truly her opinion of "The Vicar of Wakefield," and of other works, the names of which I omit (Though the names are omitted, yet the authors mentioned deserve Charlotte's approbation, and will feel it in their hearts when they read this passage. It concerns no other person.), I could no longer contain myself, but gave full utterance to what I thought of it: and it was not until Charlotte had addressed herself to the two other ladies, that I remembered their presence, and observed them sitting mute with astonishment. The aunt looked at me several times with an air of raillery, which, however, I did not at all mind.
CAN: I tried to hide how those words moved me, with little success; for when she went on to speak so truly about The Vicar of Wakefield and other books — whose names I'll leave out (the authors named deserve Charlotte's praise and will feel it in their hearts when they read this; no one else need mind) — I couldn't hold myself in any longer and said exactly what I thought. It was only when Charlotte turned to the other two ladies that I noticed they'd been sitting there wide-eyed the whole time, hearing nothing of it. The aunt gave me more than one mocking glance, which I didn't mind in the least.
--- para 14 ---
SRC: We talked of the pleasures of dancing. "If it is a fault to love it," said Charlotte, "I am ready to confess that I prize it above all other amusements. If anything disturbs me, I go to the piano, play an air to which I have danced, and all goes right again directly."
CAN: We talked of the pleasures of dancing. "If it's a fault to love it," said Charlotte, "then I freely own I prize dancing above every other amusement. Whenever something is troubling me, I go and drum out a dance I've danced on my out-of-tune piano, and at once everything is right again."
--- para 15 ---
SRC: You, who know me, can fancy how steadfastly I gazed upon her rich dark eyes during these remarks, how my very soul gloated over her warm lips and fresh, glowing cheeks, how I became quite lost in the delightful meaning of her words, so much so, that I scarcely heard the actual expressions. In short, I alighted from the carriage like a person in a dream, and was so lost to the dim world around me, that I scarcely heard the music which resounded from the illuminated ballroom.
CAN: You, who know me, can imagine how I fixed my eyes on those rich dark ones of hers as she said all this, how my whole soul hung on her warm lips and fresh, glowing cheeks, how, lost in the lovely sense of what she meant, I often hardly caught the words themselves. In short — you know me — I stepped down from the carriage like a man in a dream, so lost to the dusk-lit world around me that I barely heard the music drifting down from the lit-up ballroom.
--- para 16 ---
SRC: The two Messrs. Andran and a certain N. N. (I cannot trouble myself with the names), who were the aunt's and Charlotte's partners, received us at the carriage-door, and took possession of their ladies, whilst I followed with mine.
CAN: Two gentlemen named Audran and a certain N.N. — I can't be bothered with all the names — who were the aunt's and Charlotte's partners, met us at the carriage door and carried off their ladies, while I led mine upstairs.
--- para 17 ---
SRC: We commenced with a minuet. I led out one lady after another, and precisely those who were the most disagreeable could not bring themselves to leave off. Charlotte and her partner began an English country dance, and you must imagine my delight when it was their turn to dance the figure with us. You should see Charlotte dance. She dances with her whole heart and soul: her figure is all harmony, elegance, and grace, as if she were conscious of nothing else, and had no other thought or feeling; and, doubtless, for the moment, every other sensation is extinct.
CAN: We began with minuets. I led out one lady after another, and of course it was exactly the most tiresome ones who couldn't bring themselves to give me back my hand and have done. Charlotte and her partner started an English country dance, and you can imagine my delight when it came to her turn to do the figure with us. You ought to see Charlotte dance! She dances with her whole heart and soul; her whole body is one harmony, so carefree and easy, as if dancing were everything, as if she thought and felt nothing else — and surely in that moment everything else does fall away.
--- para 18 ---
SRC: She was engaged for the second country dance, but promised me the third, and assured me, with the most agreeable freedom, that she was very fond of waltzing. "It is the custom here," she said, "for the previous partners to waltz together; but my partner is an indifferent waltzer, and will feel delighted if I save him the trouble. Your partner is not allowed to waltz, and, indeed, is equally incapable: but I observed during the country dance that you waltz well; so, if you will waltz with me, I beg you would propose it to my partner, and I will propose it to yours." We agreed, and it was arranged that our partners should mutually entertain each other.
CAN: She was engaged for the second country dance but promised me the third, and assured me with the most charming frankness that she loved to waltz. "It's the custom here," she said, "for partners to waltz together, but mine waltzes badly and will thank me for sparing him the trouble. Your lady can't waltz and doesn't care to either, but I saw during the country dance that you waltz well; so if you'd like to waltz with me, go and ask my partner's leave, and I'll go and ask your lady's." We agreed, and arranged that her partner should keep my lady company in the meantime.
--- para 19 ---
SRC: We set off, and, at first, delighted ourselves with the usual graceful motions of the arms. With what grace, with what ease, she moved! When the waltz commenced, and the dancers whirled around each other in the giddy maze, there was some confusion, owing to the incapacity of some of the dancers. We judiciously remained still, allowing the others to weary themselves; and, when the awkward dancers had withdrawn, we joined in, and kept it up famously together with one other couple,—Andran and his partner. Never did I dance more lightly. I felt myself more than mortal, holding this loveliest of creatures in my arms, flying, with her as rapidly as the wind, till I lost sight of every other object; and O Wilhelm, I vowed at that moment, that a maiden whom I loved, or for whom I felt the slightest attachment, never, never should waltz with any one else but with me, if I went to perdition for it!—you will understand this.
CAN: Off we went, and at first delighted in the graceful play of the arms. With what grace, what lightness she moved! When the waltz proper began and the couples went wheeling round one another, there was some confusion at first, since several of the dancers weren't up to it. We sensibly held back and let them tire themselves out, and once the clumsy ones had dropped away we joined in and kept it up splendidly, along with one other couple, Audran and his partner. I have never danced so lightly in my life. I was no longer a mere man. To hold the loveliest of creatures in my arms and fly with her like the wind, until everything else around us vanished — Wilhelm, to be honest, I swore to myself in that moment that a girl I loved, that I had any claim on, should never waltz with anyone but me, though it cost me my soul. You'll understand.
--- para 20 ---
SRC: We took a few turns in the room to recover our breath. Charlotte sat down, and felt refreshed by partaking of some oranges which I had had secured,—the only ones that had been left; but at every slice which, from politeness, she offered to her neighbours, I felt as though a dagger went through my heart.
CAN: We took a few turns about the room to get our breath. Then Charlotte sat down, and the oranges I'd managed to put aside — the last ones left — did her good; only, every slice she politely offered to a greedy neighbor beside her went through my heart like a knife.
--- para 21 ---
SRC: We were the second couple in the third country dance. As we were going down (and Heaven knows with what ecstasy I gazed at her arms and eyes, beaming with the sweetest feeling of pure and genuine enjoyment), we passed a lady whom I had noticed for her charming expression of countenance; although she was no longer young. She looked at Charlotte with a smile, then, holding up her finger in a threatening attitude, repeated twice in a very significant tone of voice the name of "Albert."
CAN: In the third country dance we were the second couple. As we were going down the line — and heaven knows with what rapture I hung on her arm and her eyes, brimming with the purest, frankest delight — we passed a woman whose lovely, kindly face had caught my eye, though she was no longer young. She looked at Charlotte with a smile, raised a warning finger, and as we flew past said the name "Albert" twice, with great meaning.
--- para 22 ---
SRC: "Who is Albert," said I to Charlotte, "if it is not impertinent to ask?" She was about to answer, when we were obliged to separate, in order to execute a figure in the dance; and, as we crossed over again in front of each other, I perceived she looked somewhat pensive. "Why need I conceal it from you?" she said, as she gave me her hand for the promenade. "Albert is a worthy man, to whom I am engaged." Now, there was nothing new to me in this (for the girls had told me of it on the way); but it was so far new that I had not thought of it in connection with her whom, in so short a time, I had learned to prize so highly. Enough, I became confused, got out in the figure, and occasioned general confusion; so that it required all Charlotte's presence of mind to set me right by pulling and pushing me into my proper place.
CAN: "Who is Albert," I asked Charlotte, "if it's not rude to ask?" She was just about to answer when we had to part to make a figure, and as we crossed in front of each other again I thought she looked a little pensive. "Why should I hide it from you?" she said, giving me her hand for the promenade. "Albert is a fine man, and I'm as good as engaged to him." Now this was no news to me — the girls had told me on the way out — and yet it struck me as wholly new, because I hadn't yet thought of it in connection with her, who in so short a time had come to mean so much to me. At any rate I lost my head, lost my place in the figure, and threw the whole set into confusion, so that it took all Charlotte's composure, pulling and pushing me, to get me right again.
--- para 23 ---
SRC: The dance was not yet finished when the lightning which had for some time been seen in the horizon, and which I had asserted to proceed entirely from heat, grew more violent; and the thunder was heard above the music. When any distress or terror surprises us in the midst of our amusements, it naturally makes a deeper impression than at other times, either because the contrast makes us more keenly susceptible, or rather perhaps because our senses are then more open to impressions, and the shock is consequently stronger. To this cause I must ascribe the fright and shrieks of the ladies. One sagaciously sat down in a corner with her back to the window, and held her fingers to her ears; a second knelt down before her, and hid her face in her lap; a third threw herself between them, and embraced her sister with a thousand tears; some insisted on going home; others, unconscious of their actions, wanted sufficient presence of mind to repress the impertinence of their young partners, who sought to direct to themselves those sighs which the lips of our agitated beauties intended for heaven. Some of the gentlemen had gone down-stairs to smoke a quiet cigar, and the rest of the company gladly embraced a happy suggestion of the hostess to retire into another room which was provided with shutters and curtains. We had hardly got there, when Charlotte placed the chairs in a circle; and, when the company had sat down in compliance with her request, she forthwith proposed a round game.
CAN: The dance wasn't over when the lightning that had been flickering on the horizon for a while — which I'd insisted was only heat lightning — grew fierce, and the thunder drowned the music. When fear or alarm catches us in the middle of our pleasures it always strikes deeper than usual, whether because the contrast makes us feel it more keenly, or, more likely, because our senses are wide open just then and the shock goes in harder. To this I lay the panic and shrieks of the ladies. One cleverly sat down in a corner with her back to the window and her fingers in her ears; another knelt before her and hid her face in the first one's lap; a third pushed in between them and threw her arms around her sister, weeping buckets. Some wanted to go home; others, scarcely knowing what they did, had too little presence of mind to fend off the cheek of their young partners, who were busy intercepting for themselves the frightened prayers our trembling beauties meant for heaven. Some of the men had slipped downstairs for a quiet pipe, and the rest of us gladly took up the hostess's happy suggestion of moving into another room with shutters and curtains. We'd hardly got there when Charlotte set the chairs in a ring and, once everyone had sat down at her bidding, proposed a game.
--- para 24 ---
SRC: I noticed some of the company prepare their mouths and draw themselves up at the prospect of some agreeable forfeit. "Let us play at counting," said Charlotte. "Now, pay attention: I shall go round the circle from right to left; and each person is to count, one after the other, the number that comes to him, and must count fast; whoever stops or mistakes is to have a box on the ear, and so on, till we have counted a thousand." It was delightful to see the fun. She went round the circle with upraised arm. "One," said the first; "two," the second; "three," the third; and so on, till Charlotte went faster and faster. One made a mistake, instantly a box on the ear; and, amid the laughter that ensued, came another box; and so on, faster and faster. I myself came in for two. I fancied they were harder than the rest, and felt quite delighted. A general laughter and confusion put an end to the game long before we had counted as far as a thousand. The party broke up into little separate knots: the storm had ceased, and I followed Charlotte into the ballroom. On the way she said, "The game banished their fears of the storm." I could make no reply. "I myself," she continued, "was as much frightened as any of them; but by affecting courage, to keep up the spirits of the others, I forgot my apprehensions." We went to the window. It was still thundering at a distance: a soft rain was pouring down over the country, and filled the air around us with delicious odours. Charlotte leaned forward on her arm; her eyes wandered over the scene; she raised them to the sky, and then turned them upon me; they were moistened with tears; she placed her hand on mine and said, "Klopstock!" at once I remembered the magnificent ode which was in her thoughts: I felt oppressed with the weight of my sensations, and sank under them. It was more than I could bear. I bent over her hand, kissed it in a stream of delicious tears, and again looked up to her eyes. Divine Klopstock! why didst thou not see thy apotheosis in those eyes? And thy name so often profaned, would that I never heard it repeated!
CAN: I saw more than one person pucker up and stretch in pleasant anticipation of a forfeit. "Let's play counting," said Charlotte. "Now pay attention.
