I'll go round the circle from right to left, and each of you in turn calls out his number — count quickly; whoever hesitates or slips gets a box on the ear, and so on up to a thousand." It was a joy to watch. She went round the ring with her arm raised. "One," said the first; "two," the next; "three," the third; and so on, while she went faster and faster. Then someone slipped — smack, a box on the ear — and amid the laughter the next one slipped too, and so on, faster and faster. I caught two boxes myself, and was delighted to fancy they were harder than the rest. A general burst of laughter and uproar broke the game off well before we reached a thousand. The party drifted apart into little knots; the storm had passed, and I followed Charlotte back into the ballroom. On the way she said, "The forfeits made them forget all about the storm." I couldn't say a word. "I was as frightened as any of them," she went on, "but by acting brave to keep the others' courage up I forgot my own fear." We went to the window. Thunder was still rolling in the distance; a soft rain was falling over the land, and the most delicious fragrance rose to us on the warm air. She stood there leaning on her elbow, her gaze searching the countryside; she looked up at the sky and then at me, her eyes full of tears; she laid her hand on mine and said, "Klopstock!" At once I knew the magnificent ode in her mind, and I sank under the flood of feeling that the one word loosed in me. It was more than I could bear. I bent over her hand and kissed it through tears of bliss, and looked again into her eyes. Noble poet! If only you could have seen your worship in that look — and may I never again hear your name, so often profaned, on lesser lips!

=== Ch 13: June 19, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I no longer remember where I stopped in my narrative: I only know it was two in the morning when I went to bed; and if you had been with me, that I might have talked instead of writing to you, I should, in all probability, have kept you up till daylight.
CAN: I no longer know where I broke off the story; all I know is that it was two in the morning when I got to bed, and that if I could have talked to you instead of writing, I'd very likely have kept you up till dawn.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: I think I have not yet related what happened as we rode home from the ball, nor have I time to tell you now. It was a most magnificent sunrise: the whole country was refreshed, and the rain fell drop by drop from the trees in the forest. Our companions were asleep. Charlotte asked me if I did not wish to sleep also, and begged of me not to make any ceremony on her account. Looking steadfastly at her, I answered, "As long as I see those eyes open, there is no fear of my falling asleep." We both continued awake till we reached her door. The maid opened it softly, and assured her, in answer to her inquiries, that her father and the children were well, and still sleeping. I left her asking permission to visit her in the course of the day. She consented, and I went, and, since that time, sun, moon, and stars may pursue their course: I know not whether it is day or night; the whole world is nothing to me.
CAN: I don't think I've told you yet what happened on the drive home from the ball — and I haven't time now either. It was the most glorious sunrise: the dripping wood, the freshened country all around. Our companions had dozed off. Charlotte asked whether I didn't want to sleep too, and begged me not to stay awake on her account. "As long as I can see those eyes open," I said, looking straight at her, "there's no danger of my falling asleep." We both stayed awake right to her door. The maid let us in softly and assured her, in answer to her questions, that her father and the children were well and still asleep. I left her, asking leave to call on her later that same day. She gave it, and I went; and since then sun, moon, and stars may go about their business as they please — I don't know whether it's day or night, and the whole world has melted away around me.

=== Ch 14: June 21, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: My days are as happy as those reserved by God for his elect; and, whatever be my fate hereafter, I can never say that I have not tasted joy,—the purest joy of life. You know Walheim. I am now completely settled there. In that spot I am only half a league from Charlotte; and there I enjoy myself, and taste all the pleasure which can fall to the lot of man.
CAN: My days are as happy as the ones God keeps for his saints; and whatever becomes of me, no one can ever say I haven't known joy — the purest joy of life. You know Walheim. I'm fully settled there now. From there it's only half a league to Charlotte; there I feel myself, and taste all the happiness a man can be given.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: Little did I imagine, when I selected Walheim for my pedestrian excursions, that all heaven lay so near it. How often in my wanderings from the hillside or from the meadows across the river, have I beheld this hunting-lodge, which now contains within it all the joy of my heart!
CAN: Little did I think, when I picked Walheim as the goal of my walks, that heaven itself lay so close by. How often, on my long rambles — from the hilltop, from the meadow across the river — have I looked out at that hunting lodge, which now holds everything my heart could wish.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: I have often, my dear Wilhelm, reflected on the eagerness men feel to wander and make new discoveries, and upon that secret impulse which afterward inclines them to return to their narrow circle, conform to the laws of custom, and embarrass themselves no longer with what passes around them.
CAN: I've often thought, dear Wilhelm, about the longing that drives men to roam and seek out new things, and then about the quiet pull that draws them back afterward to their narrow round, to fall in with custom and trouble themselves no more about the wider world.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: It is so strange how, when I came here first, and gazed upon that lovely valley from the hillside, I felt charmed with the entire scene surrounding me. The little wood opposite—how delightful to sit under its shade! How fine the view from that point of rock! Then, that delightful chain of hills, and the exquisite valleys at their feet! Could I but wander and lose myself amongst them! I went, and returned without finding what I wished. Distance, my friend, is like futurity. A dim vastness is spread before our souls: the perceptions of our mind are as obscure as those of our vision; and we desire earnestly to surrender up our whole being, that it may be filled with the complete and perfect bliss of one glorious emotion. But alas! when we have attained our object, when the distant there becomes the present here, all is changed: we are as poor and circumscribed as ever, and our souls still languish for unattainable happiness.
CAN: It's so strange: when I first came here and looked out over the lovely valley from the hill, how it all drew me — the little wood there, how good to sit in its shade; that peak, what a view from it; that chain of gentle hills and the snug valleys at their feet — oh, to lose myself wandering among them! And I went, and came back, and hadn't found what I'd hoped. Distance, my friend, is like the future. A great dim whole lies spread before our soul; our feeling blurs into it as our sight does, and we long to give our whole self up and be filled to the brim with the bliss of one single, vast, glorious feeling. But alas — when we hurry toward it, when there becomes here, everything is just as it was, and we stand as poor and hemmed-in as ever, our soul thirsting still for the happiness that slipped away.
--- para 4 ---
SRC: So does the restless traveller pant for his native soil, and find in his own cottage, in the arms of his wife, in the affections of his children, and in the labour necessary for their support, that happiness which he had sought in vain through the wide world.
CAN: So even the most restless wanderer comes in the end to long for home, and finds in his own cottage, on his wife's breast, among his children, and in the work of providing for them, the happiness he chased in vain across the wide world.
--- para 5 ---
SRC: When, in the morning at sunrise, I go out to Walheim, and with my own hands gather in the garden the pease which are to serve for my dinner, when I sit down to shell them, and read my Homer during the intervals, and then, selecting a saucepan from the kitchen, fetch my own butter, put my mess on the fire, cover it up, and sit down to stir it as occasion requires, I figure to myself the illustrious suitors of Penelope, killing, dressing, and preparing their own oxen and swine. Nothing fills me with a more pure and genuine sense of happiness than those traits of patriarchal life which, thank Heaven! I can imitate without affectation. Happy is it, indeed, for me that my heart is capable of feeling the same simple and innocent pleasure as the peasant whose table is covered with food of his own rearing, and who not only enjoys his meal, but remembers with delight the happy days and sunny mornings when he planted it, the soft evenings when he watered it, and the pleasure he experienced in watching its daily growth.
CAN: When I walk out to Walheim at sunrise and there in the garden pick my own peas for dinner, and sit down to shell them while I read my Homer between handfuls; when I choose a pot in the little kitchen, cut myself a pat of butter, set my peas on the fire, cover them, and sit by to give them a stir now and then — I can feel so vividly how Penelope's overbearing suitors slaughtered and carved and roasted their own oxen and swine. Nothing fills me with so quiet and true a sense of happiness as these touches of patriarchal life, which, thank heaven, I can weave into my own without affectation. How glad I am that my heart can feel the simple, harmless joy of a man who brings to his table a cabbage he grew himself, and who in that one moment relives not just the cabbage but all the good days — the fine morning he planted it, the soft evenings he watered it, the pleasure he took in watching it grow — all of it together again.

=== Ch 15: June 29, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: The day before yesterday, the physician came from the town to pay a visit to the judge. He found me on the floor playing with Charlotte's children. Some of them were scrambling over me, and others romped with me; and, as I caught and tickled them, they made a great noise. The doctor is a formal sort of personage: he adjusts the plaits of his ruffles, and continually settles his frill whilst he is talking to you; and he thought my conduct beneath the dignity of a sensible man. I could perceive this by his countenance. But I did not suffer myself to be disturbed. I allowed him to continue his wise conversation, whilst I rebuilt the children's card houses for them as fast as they threw them down. He went about the town afterward, complaining that the judge's children were spoiled enough before, but that now Werther was completely ruining them.
CAN: The day before yesterday the doctor came out from town to see the steward and found me on the floor among Charlotte's children, some of them climbing over me, others teasing me, while I tickled them and we all made a great racket. The doctor is a very stiff sort of man; he keeps adjusting the pleats of his cuffs and tweaking his frill while he talks to you, and he plainly thought such carrying-on beneath the dignity of a sensible man — I could read it in his face. But I didn't let it put me off; I let him go on with his weighty talk while I rebuilt the children's card houses for them as fast as they knocked them down. And sure enough he went round town afterward complaining that the steward's children were spoiled enough already, but that now Werther was ruining them altogether.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: Yes, my dear Wilhelm, nothing on this earth affects my heart so much as children. When I look on at their doings; when I mark in the little creatures the seeds of all those virtues and qualities which they will one day find so indispensable; when I behold in the obstinate all the future firmness and constancy of a noble character; in the capricious, that levity and gaiety of temper which will carry them lightly over the dangers and troubles of life, their whole nature simple and unpolluted,—then I call to mind the golden words of the Great Teacher of mankind, "Unless ye become like one of these!" And now, my friend, these children, who are our equals, whom we ought to consider as our models, we treat them as though they were our subjects. They are allowed no will of their own. And have we, then, none ourselves? Whence comes our exclusive right? Is it because we are older and more experienced? Great God! from the height of thy heaven thou beholdest great children and little children, and no others; and thy Son has long since declared which afford thee greatest pleasure. But they believe in him, and hear him not,—that, too, is an old story; and they train their children after their own image, etc.
CAN: Yes, dear Wilhelm, nothing on this earth touches my heart like children. When I watch them, and see in the little creatures the seeds of every virtue, every strength they'll one day so badly need; when I make out in the stubborn child the firmness and steadiness of a fine character to come, and in the unruly one the good humor and lightness that will carry him safely over life's dangers — and all of it still whole, still unspoiled — then I say over to myself again and again the golden words of the Teacher of mankind: "Unless you become as one of these!" And yet, my friend, these children, who are our equals, whom we ought to take as our models, we treat as our subjects. They're to have no will of their own. And have we none, then? Where does our great privilege come from? Because we're older and wiser? Good God in heaven, you see only big children and little children, and nothing else; and your Son said long ago which give you the greater joy. But they believe in him and don't listen to him — that too is an old story — and they shape their children after themselves, and so on.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: Adieu, Wilhelm: I will not further bewilder myself with this subject.
CAN: Goodbye, Wilhelm; I won't muddle my head any further with this.

=== Ch 16: July 1, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: The consolation Charlotte can bring to an invalid I experience from my own heart, which suffers more from her absence than many a poor creature lingering on a bed of sickness. She is gone to spend a few days in the town with a very worthy woman, who is given over by the physicians, and wishes to have Charlotte near her in her last moments. I accompanied her last week on a visit to the Vicar of S—, a small village in the mountains, about a league hence. We arrived about four o'clock: Charlotte had taken her little sister with her. When we entered the vicarage court, we found the good old man sitting on a bench before the door, under the shade of two large walnut-trees. At the sight of Charlotte he seemed to gain new life, rose, forgot his stick, and ventured to walk toward her. She ran to him, and made him sit down again; then, placing herself by his side, she gave him a number of messages from her father, and then caught up his youngest child, a dirty, ugly little thing, the joy of his old age, and kissed it. I wish you could have witnessed her attention to this old man,—how she raised her voice on account of his deafness; how she told him of healthy young people, who had been carried off when it was least expected; praised the virtues of Carlsbad, and commended his determination to spend the ensuing summer there; and assured him that he looked better and stronger than he did when she saw him last. I, in the meantime, paid attention to his good lady. The old man seemed quite in spirits; and as I could not help admiring the beauty of the walnut-trees, which formed such an agreeable shade over our heads, he began, though with some little difficulty, to tell us their history. "As to the oldest," said he, "we do not know who planted it,—some say one clergyman, and some another: but the younger one, there behind us, is exactly the age of my wife, fifty years old next October; her father planted it in the morning, and in the evening she came into the world. My wife's father was my predecessor here, and I cannot tell you how fond he was of that tree; and it is fully as dear to me. Under the shade of that very tree, upon a log of wood, my wife was seated knitting, when I, a poor student, came into this court for the first time, just seven and twenty years ago." Charlotte inquired for his daughter. He said she was gone with Herr Schmidt to the meadows, and was with the haymakers. The old man then resumed his story, and told us how his predecessor had taken a fancy to him, as had his daughter likewise; and how he had become first his curate, and subsequently his successor. He had scarcely finished his story when his daughter returned through the garden, accompanied by the above-mentioned Herr Schmidt. She welcomed Charlotte affectionately, and I confess I was much taken with her appearance. She was a lively-looking, good-humoured brunette, quite competent to amuse one for a short time in the country. Her lover (for such Herr Schmidt evidently appeared to be) was a polite, reserved personage, and would not join our conversation, notwithstanding all Charlotte's endeavours to draw him out. I was much annoyed at observing, by his countenance, that his silence did not arise from want of talent, but from caprice and ill-humour. This subsequently became very evident, when we set out to take a walk, and Frederica joining Charlotte, with whom I was talking, the worthy gentleman's face, which was naturally rather sombre, became so dark and angry that Charlotte was obliged to touch my arm, and remind me that I was talking too much to Frederica. Nothing distresses me more than to see men torment each other; particularly when in the flower of their age, in the very season of pleasure, they waste their few short days of sunshine in quarrels and disputes, and only perceive their error when it is too late to repair it. This thought dwelt upon my mind; and in the evening, when we returned to the vicar's, and were sitting round the table with our bread and milk, the conversation turned on the joys and sorrows of the world, I could not resist the temptation to inveigh bitterly against ill-humour. "We are apt," said I, "to complain, but—with very little cause, that our happy days are few, and our evil days many. If our hearts were always disposed to receive the benefits Heaven sends us, we should acquire strength to support evil when it comes." "But," observed the vicar's wife, "we cannot always command our tempers, so much depends upon the constitution: when the body suffers, the mind is ill at ease." "I acknowledge that," I continued; "but we must consider such a disposition in the light of a disease, and inquire whether there is no remedy for it."
CAN: The comfort Charlotte can bring to a sick friend, I feel in my own heart, which suffers more from being apart from her than many a poor soul wasting away in bed. She's spending a few days in town with a worthy woman whom the doctors have given up, and who wants Charlotte near her at the end. Last week I went with her to visit the vicar of St—, a little village up in the hills about a league from here. We got there around four; Charlotte had brought her little sister along. As we came into the vicarage yard we found the good old man sitting on a bench by the door under the shade of two big walnut trees. At the sight of Charlotte he seemed to come to life, forgot his stick, and made to get up and walk toward her. She ran to him and made him sit down again, took a seat beside him, passed on a great many messages from her father, and then scooped up his youngest, an ugly, grubby little thing, the darling of his old age, and kissed it. I wish you could have seen how she gave herself to that old man — raising her voice for his deafness, telling him of strong young people carried off without warning, praising the waters at Carlsbad, approving his plan to spend the coming summer there, telling him he looked better and stronger than the last time she saw him. I, meanwhile, paid my respects to his wife. The old man grew quite cheerful, and since I couldn't help admiring the fine walnut trees that shaded us so pleasantly, he began, with a little effort, to tell us their story. "The older one," he said, "we don't know who planted — some say this parson, some that. But the younger one behind us is exactly my wife's age: fifty next October. Her father planted it the morning of the day she was born that evening. He was my predecessor here, and there's no telling how he loved that tree — and it's just as dear to me. My wife was sitting under it on a beam, knitting, when I first walked into this yard, a poor student, twenty-seven years ago." Charlotte asked after his daughter, and he said she'd gone out to the meadows with Herr Schmidt to see the haymakers, and then went on with his story: how his predecessor had taken a liking to him, and the daughter too, and how he'd become first the man's curate and then his successor. He'd barely finished when the daughter came back through the garden with this Herr Schmidt. She gave Charlotte a warm welcome, and I'll admit I rather liked her — a lively, good-natured brunette, the sort who could keep you amused for a while in the country. Her suitor (for that's plainly what Schmidt was) was a polite but reserved fellow who wouldn't join our talk however hard Charlotte tried to draw him out. What annoyed me most was seeing in his face that it was temper, not dullness, that kept him silent. This came out clearly later: when we set off on a walk and Frederica fell in beside Charlotte, and so beside me, the man's face, naturally rather dark, grew so black and sullen that Charlotte had to pluck my sleeve and warn me I was paying Frederica too much attention. Now nothing in the world vexes me more than to see people torment one another — above all when the young, in the very flower of life, in the very season for joy, throw away their few bright days on quarrels, and only see what they've done when it's too late to mend it. It weighed on me, and that evening, back at the vicarage and sitting round the table over our bread and milk, when the talk turned to the joys and sorrows of the world, I couldn't help laying into ill-humor. "We're forever complaining," I said, "that good days are so few and bad days so many — and usually with little reason. If our hearts were always open to take in the good that heaven sends us, we'd have the strength to bear the bad when it comes." "But we can't command our moods," said the vicar's wife. "So much depends on the body; when you're out of sorts physically, nothing feels right." "Granted," I said, "but then let's treat it as an illness and ask whether there isn't a cure."
--- para 1 ---
SRC: "I should be glad to hear one," said Charlotte: "at least, I think very much depends upon ourselves; I know it is so with me. When anything annoys me, and disturbs my temper, I hasten into the garden, hum a couple of country dances, and it is all right with me directly." "That is what I meant," I replied; "ill-humour resembles indolence: it is natural to us; but if once we have courage to exert ourselves, we find our work run fresh from our hands, and we experience in the activity from which we shrank a real enjoyment." Frederica listened very attentively: and the young man objected, that we were not masters of ourselves, and still less so of our feelings. "The question is about a disagreeable feeling," I added, "from which every one would willingly escape, but none know their own power without trial. Invalids are glad to consult physicians, and submit to the most scrupulous regimen, the most nauseous medicines, in order to recover their health." I observed that the good old man inclined his head, and exerted himself to hear our discourse; so I raised my voice, and addressed myself directly to him. "We preach against a great many crimes," I observed, "but I never remember a sermon delivered against ill-humour." "That may do very well for your town clergymen," said he: "country people are never ill-humoured; though, indeed, it might be useful, occasionally, to my wife for instance, and the judge." We all laughed, as did he likewise very cordially, till he fell into a fit of coughing, which interrupted our conversation for a time. Herr Schmidt resumed the subject. "You call ill humour a crime," he remarked, "but I think you use too strong a term." "Not at all," I replied, "if that deserves the name which is so pernicious to ourselves and our neighbours. Is it not enough that we want the power to make one another happy, must we deprive each other of the pleasure which we can all make for ourselves? Show me the man who has the courage to hide his ill-humour, who bears the whole burden himself, without disturbing the peace of those around him. No: ill-humour arises from an inward consciousness of our own want of merit, from a discontent which ever accompanies that envy which foolish vanity engenders. We see people happy, whom we have not made so, and cannot endure the sight." Charlotte looked at me with a smile; she observed the emotion with which I spoke: and a tear in the eyes of Frederica stimulated me to proceed. "Woe unto those," I said, "who use their power over a human heart to destroy the simple pleasures it would naturally enjoy! All the favours, all the attentions, in the world cannot compensate for the loss of that happiness which a cruel tyranny has destroyed." My heart was full as I spoke. A recollection of many things which had happened pressed upon my mind, and filled my eyes with tears. "We should daily repeat to ourselves," I exclaimed, "that we should not interfere with our friends, unless to leave them in possession of their own joys, and increase their happiness by sharing it with them! But when their souls are tormented by a violent passion, or their hearts rent with grief, is it in your power to afford them the slightest consolation?
CAN: "That makes sense," said Charlotte. "At least I think a great deal depends on us; I know it does with me. When something nettles me and threatens to sour my mood, I jump up and pace the garden singing a couple of dance tunes, and it's gone at once." "That's just what I meant," I said. "Ill-humor is exactly like idleness — it comes naturally to us — but once we find the courage to rouse ourselves, the work flies along and we discover a real pleasure in the activity we'd been shrinking from." Frederica was listening closely, and the young man objected that we aren't masters of ourselves, still less of our feelings. "We're talking about a disagreeable feeling," I answered, "that everyone would be glad to escape — and no one knows how far his own strength reaches until he tries. Surely a sick man will gladly consult every doctor, and not refuse the strictest diet or the bitterest medicine, to get his health back." I noticed the good old vicar leaning in, straining to follow, so I raised my voice and turned to him directly. "We preach against all sorts of vices," I said, "but I've never yet heard a sermon against ill-humor." "The town clergy might manage that," he said; "country folk are never out of temper — though now and then it mightn't hurt, as a tonic for my wife, say, and the steward." We all laughed, and he laughed heartily too, until a coughing fit took him and broke off our talk for a while. Then Herr Schmidt picked it up again. "You call ill-humor a vice," he said, "but I think that's putting it too strongly." "Not at all," I replied, "if it deserves the name for the harm it does us and those around us. Isn't it enough that we can't make one another happy — must we also rob each other of the little happiness each heart might find on its own? Show me the man so out of temper who has the strength to hide it, to carry it alone without poisoning the peace of everyone near him! No — ill-humor springs from an inward sense of our own unworthiness, from a discontent forever tangled up with the envy that foolish vanity breeds. We see people happy whom we didn't make so, and we can't stand it." Charlotte smiled at me, seeing the feeling I spoke with, and a tear in Frederica's eye spurred me on. "Woe to those," I said, "who use their power over another heart to rob it of the simple joys it would have had on its own! All the gifts and attentions in the world can't buy back one moment's pleasure that a jealous tyranny has spoiled at its source." My heart was full as I spoke; the memory of much that had happened pressed on me and brought tears to my eyes. "We should tell ourselves every day," I cried, "that we owe our friends nothing but to leave them their own joys and to deepen their happiness by sharing it. When their souls are racked by some violent passion, or torn with grief, can you give them one drop of comfort?
--- para 2 ---
SRC: "And when the last fatal malady seizes the being whose untimely grave you have prepared, when she lies languid and exhausted before you, her dim eyes raised to heaven, and the damp of death upon her pallid brow, there you stand at her bedside like a condemned criminal, with the bitter feeling that your whole fortune could not save her; and the agonising thought wrings you, that all your efforts are powerless to impart even a moment's strength to the departing soul, or quicken her with a transitory consolation."
CAN: "And when the last fatal illness fastens on the woman whose early grave you helped dig, and she lies there spent and failing, her dim eyes turned to heaven and the sweat of death on her pale forehead — you stand at the foot of the bed like a condemned man, knowing in your bones that all you have could do nothing to save her, wrung by the thought that nothing in your power can pour even a flicker of strength into the sinking soul, one last spark of comfort."
--- para 3 ---
SRC: At these words the remembrance of a similar scene at which I had been once present fell with full force upon my heart. I buried my face in my handkerchief, and hastened from the room, and was only recalled to my recollection by Charlotte's voice, who reminded me that it was time to return home. With what tenderness she chid me on the way for the too eager interest I took in everything! She declared it would do me injury, and that I ought to spare myself. Yes, my angel! I will do so for your sake.
CAN: At those words the memory of just such a scene I'd once stood through broke over me with full force. I pressed my handkerchief to my eyes and hurried out of the room, and only came back to myself at Charlotte's voice calling that it was time to go home. And how she scolded me on the way, so tenderly, for taking everything too much to heart — that it would be the ruin of me, that I must spare myself! Oh my angel — for your sake I will.

=== Ch 17: July 6, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: She is still with her dying friend, and is still the same bright, beautiful creature whose presence softens pain, and sheds happiness around whichever way she turns. She went out yesterday with her little sisters: I knew it, and went to meet them; and we walked together. In about an hour and a half we returned to the town. We stopped at the spring I am so fond of, and which is now a thousand times dearer to me than ever. Charlotte seated herself upon the low wall, and we gathered about her. I looked around, and recalled the time when my heart was unoccupied and free. "Dear fountain!" I said, "since that time I have no more come to enjoy cool repose by thy fresh stream: I have passed thee with careless steps, and scarcely bestowed a glance upon thee." I looked down, and observed Charlotte's little sister, Jane, coming up the steps with a glass of water. I turned toward Charlotte, and I felt her influence over me. Jane at the moment approached with the glass. Her sister, Marianne, wished to take it from her. "No!" cried the child, with the sweetest expression of face, "Charlotte must drink first."
CAN: She's still with her dying friend, and still the same bright, lovely creature who eases pain and scatters happiness wherever she turns. Yesterday evening she went walking with her little sisters; I knew it, met them, and we went on together. After about an hour and a half we turned back toward town. We stopped at the spring I love so much — now a thousand times dearer to me than before. Charlotte sat down on the low wall and we gathered round her. I looked about me, and the time when my heart was still free and empty came back to me. "Dear spring," I said, "since then I've never once come to rest by your cool water; sometimes, hurrying past, I haven't even glanced at you." I looked down and saw Charlotte's little sister Jane coming up the steps with a glass of water. I turned to Charlotte, and felt all over again what she is to me. Just then Jane came up with the glass, and her sister Marianne tried to take it from her. "No!" the child cried, with the sweetest look, "no — Charlotte must drink first!"
--- para 1 ---
SRC: The affection and simplicity with which this was uttered so charmed me, that I sought to express my feelings by catching up the child and kissing her heartily. She was frightened, and began to cry. "You should not do that," said Charlotte: I felt perplexed. "Come, Jane," she continued, taking her hand, and leading her down the steps again, "it is no matter: wash yourself quickly in the fresh water." I stood and watched them; and when I saw the little dear rubbing her cheeks with her wet hands, in full belief that all the impurities contracted from my ugly beard would be washed off by the miraculous water, and how, though Charlotte said it would do, she continued still to wash with all her might, as though she thought too much were better than too little, I assure you, Wilhelm, I never attended a baptism with greater reverence; and, when Charlotte came up from the well, I could have prostrated myself as before the prophet of an Eastern nation.
CAN: The affection, the simplicity in the way she said it carried me away so that I couldn't find any way to show what I felt but to snatch the child up and kiss her warmly — at which she screamed and burst into tears. "You shouldn't have done that," said Charlotte. I stood there at a loss. "Come, Jane," she went on, taking her by the hand and leading her down the steps, "never mind — quick, wash it off in the fresh water, quick." I stood watching as the little thing scrubbed away at her cheeks with her wet hands, certain that the miraculous water would rinse off every trace of contamination from my ugly stubble; and though Charlotte said that was enough, she kept scrubbing with all her might, as if too much were safer than too little. I tell you, Wilhelm, I never stood at a baptism with more reverence; and when Charlotte came back up I could have bowed down before her as before a prophet who had washed away a nation's sins.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: In the evening I would not resist telling the story to a person who, I thought, possessed some natural feeling, because he was a man of understanding. But what a mistake I made. He maintained it was very wrong of Charlotte, that we should not deceive children, that such things occasioned countless mistakes and superstitions, from which we were bound to protect the young. It occurred to me then, that this very man had been baptised only a week before; so I said nothing further, but maintained the justice of my own convictions. We should deal with children as God deals with us, we are happiest under the influence of innocent delusions.
CAN: That evening, in the joy of my heart, I couldn't keep from telling the story to a man I credited with some natural feeling, since he has a good head on him. But how wrong I was! He held that Charlotte had done quite wrong — that we mustn't fool children, that such things lead to no end of errors and superstitions we're bound to guard the young against. It struck me just then that the man had had a child christened only the week before, so I let it drop, and in my heart held to the truth: we ought to deal with children as God deals with us, who makes us happiest of all when he lets us reel along inside a pleasant illusion.

=== Ch 18: July 8, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: What a child is man that he should be so solicitous about a look! What a child is man! We had been to Walheim: the ladies went in a carriage; but during our walk I thought I saw in Charlotte's dark eyes—I am a fool—but forgive me! you should see them,—those eyes.—However, to be brief (for my own eyes are weighed down with sleep), you must know, when the ladies stepped into their carriage again, young W. Seldstadt, Andran, and I were standing about the door. They are a merry set of fellows, and they were all laughing and joking together. I watched Charlotte's eyes. They wandered from one to the other; but they did not light on me, on me, who stood there motionless, and who saw nothing but her! My heart bade her a thousand times adieu, but she noticed me not. The carriage drove off; and my eyes filled with tears. I looked after her: suddenly I saw Charlotte's bonnet leaning out of the window, and she turned to look back, was it at me? My dear friend, I know not; and in this uncertainty I find consolation. Perhaps she turned to look at me. Perhaps! Good-night—what a child I am!
CAN: What a child a man is, to set such store by a glance! What a child a man is! We'd gone out to Walheim. The ladies drove, and during our walk I thought I saw, in Charlotte's dark eyes — I'm a fool, forgive me, but you should see them, those eyes! To keep it short, since my own eyes are heavy with sleep: well, the ladies were getting back into the carriage, and young W., Selstadt, Audran, and I stood about the door. They're a lively lot, all laughing and bantering. I watched Charlotte's eyes; they went from one to another — but not to me, to me, who stood there fixed on her and nothing else! My heart said goodbye to her a thousand times, and she never looked at me. The carriage rolled off, and tears stood in my eyes. I gazed after it — and then I saw Charlotte's bonnet lean out of the window, and she turned to look back. At me? Ah, dear friend, I can't be sure, and in that not knowing lies my comfort. Perhaps she turned for me. Perhaps! Goodnight. Oh, what a child I am!

=== Ch 19: July 10, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: You should see how foolish I look in company when her name is mentioned, particularly when I am asked plainly how I like her. How I like her! I detest the phrase. What sort of creature must he be who merely liked Charlotte, whose whole heart and senses were not entirely absorbed by her. Like her! Some one asked me lately how I liked Ossian.
CAN: You should see what a fool I cut in company when her name comes up — especially when someone asks me point-blank how I like her. Like her! I loathe the word. What kind of man could merely like Charlotte, and not have his whole heart and senses swallowed up by her? Like her! Someone asked me the other day how I "like" Ossian!

=== Ch 20: July 11, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Madame M— is very ill. I pray for her recovery, because Charlotte shares my sufferings. I see her occasionally at my friend's house, and to-day she has told me the strangest circumstance. Old M— is a covetous, miserly fellow, who has long worried and annoyed the poor lady sadly; but she has borne her afflictions patiently. A few days ago, when the physician informed us that her recovery was hopeless, she sent for her husband (Charlotte was present), and addressed him thus: "I have something to confess, which, after my decease, may occasion trouble and confusion. I have hitherto conducted your household as frugally and economically as possible, but you must pardon me for having defrauded you for thirty years. At the commencement of our married life, you allowed a small sum for the wants of the kitchen, and the other household expenses. When our establishment increased and our property grew larger, I could not persuade you to increase the weekly allowance in proportion: in short, you know, that, when our wants were greatest, you required me to supply everything with seven florins a week. I took the money from you without an observation, but made up the weekly deficiency from the money-chest; as nobody would suspect your wife of robbing the household bank. But I have wasted nothing, and should have been content to meet my eternal Judge without this confession, if she, upon whom the management of your establishment will devolve after my decease, would be free from embarrassment upon your insisting that the allowance made to me, your former wife, was sufficient."
CAN: Madame M— is very ill. I pray she'll recover, because Charlotte suffers along with her. I see Charlotte now and then at my friend's house, and today she told me the strangest thing. Old M— is a grasping, tight-fisted fellow who has long pinched and plagued the poor woman, though she's borne it patiently. A few days ago, when the doctor told us there was no hope for her, she sent for her husband — Charlotte was in the room — and said to him: "I have something to confess that could cause trouble and confusion after I'm gone. I've kept your house as thriftily and carefully as I could all these years — but you'll have to forgive me for cheating you for thirty of them. When we were first married you set aside a small sum for the kitchen and the other household needs. As our household grew and our means with it, I could never get you to raise the weekly housekeeping to match; you know that even when our needs were greatest you insisted I run everything on seven florins a week. I took the money without a word and made up the difference each week out of the cash box, since no one would suspect the mistress of robbing the till. I've wasted nothing, and I could have gone to meet my Maker without saying any of this — except that whoever takes over the house after me won't know how to manage, and you'll keep insisting that your first wife got by on the same sum."
--- para 1 ---
SRC: I talked with Charlotte of the inconceivable manner in which men allow themselves to be blinded; how any one could avoid suspecting some deception, when seven florins only were allowed to defray expenses twice as great. But I have myself known people who believed, without any visible astonishment, that their house possessed the prophet's never-failing cruse of oil.
CAN: I talked it over with Charlotte — how unbelievably men can blind themselves, that anyone could fail to suspect something was wrong when seven florins were meant to cover costs twice that. But I've known people myself who'd have taken the prophet's never-emptying jar of oil into their own house without batting an eye.

=== Ch 21: July 13, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: No, I am not deceived. In her dark eyes I read a genuine interest in me and in my fortunes. Yes, I feel it; and I may believe my own heart which tells me—dare I say it?—dare I pronounce the divine words?—that she loves me!
CAN: No, I'm not deceiving myself. In her dark eyes I read a genuine feeling for me and for what becomes of me. Yes — I feel it, and in this I dare trust my own heart — that she — oh, dare I speak it, dare I say the words that hold heaven in them? — that she loves me!
--- para 1 ---
SRC: That she loves me! How the idea exalts me in my own eyes! And, as you can understand my feelings, I may say to you, how I honour myself since she loves me!
CAN: That she loves me! And how precious I have become to myself, how — to you I can say it, you'll understand such things — how I worship myself, since she loves me!
--- para 2 ---
SRC: Is this presumption, or is it a consciousness of the truth? I do not know a man able to supplant me in the heart of Charlotte; and yet when she speaks of her betrothed with so much warmth and affection, I feel like the soldier who has been stripped of his honours and titles, and deprived of his sword.
CAN: Is this presumption, or a true sense of how things stand? I don't know the man I'd fear as a rival in Charlotte's heart; and yet, when she speaks of her betrothed with such warmth, such affection, I feel like a soldier stripped of his rank and honors and stripped of his sword.

=== Ch 22: July 16, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: How my heart beats when by accident I touch her finger, or my feet meet hers under the table! I draw back as if from a furnace; but a secret force impels me forward again, and my senses become disordered. Her innocent, unconscious heart never knows what agony these little familiarities inflict upon me. Sometimes when we are talking she lays her hand upon mine, and in the eagerness of conversation comes closer to me, and her balmy breath reaches my lips,—when I feel as if lightning had struck me, and that I could sink into the earth. And yet, Wilhelm, with all this heavenly confidence,—if I know myself, and should ever dare—you understand me. No, no! my heart is not so corrupt, it is weak, weak enough but is not that a degree of corruption?
CAN: How my heart pounds when by chance my finger brushes hers, or our feet meet under the table! I pull back as if from a furnace, and a secret pull draws me forward again, and my head swims. Oh, her innocent, open heart has no idea what torment these little intimacies put me through. And when, in the thick of talk, she lays her hand on mine and leans closer to me, so that the heavenly breath of her mouth reaches my lips — I feel as if struck by lightning, ready to drop where I stand. And yet, Wilhelm, if I ever dared — with all this heaven, all this trust — if I ever dared to — you understand me. No, no — my heart isn't so far gone. Weak — yes, weak enough. And isn't that already a kind of corruption?
--- para 1 ---
SRC: She is to me a sacred being. All passion is still in her presence: I cannot express my sensations when I am near her. I feel as if my soul beat in every nerve of my body. There is a melody which she plays on the piano with angelic skill,—so simple is it, and yet so spiritual! It is her favourite air; and, when she plays the first note, all pain, care, and sorrow disappear from me in a moment.
CAN: She is sacred to me. Every desire falls silent in her presence. I never know what I feel when I'm near her; it's as if my soul turned over in every nerve of my body. There's a melody she plays on the piano with the touch of an angel — so simple and so full of spirit! It's her favorite tune, and the moment she strikes the first note all my pain, confusion, and brooding lift away.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: I believe every word that is said of the magic of ancient music. How her simple song enchants me! Sometimes, when I am ready to commit suicide, she sings that air; and instantly the gloom and madness which hung over me are dispersed, and I breathe freely again.
CAN: Not a word that's ever been said about the old magic of music seems unlikely to me now. How that simple song takes hold of me! And how well she knows when to play it — often just when I feel like putting a bullet through my head. The darkness and madness in me scatter, and I breathe more freely again.

=== Ch 23: July 18, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Wilhelm, what is the world to our hearts without love? What is a magic-lantern without light? You have but to kindle the flame within, and the brightest figures shine on the white wall; and, if love only show us fleeting shadows, we are yet happy, when, like mere children, we behold them, and are transported with the splendid phantoms. I have not been able to see Charlotte to-day. I was prevented by company from which I could not disengage myself. What was to be done? I sent my servant to her house, that I might at least see somebody to-day who had been near her. Oh, the impatience with which I waited for his return! the joy with which I welcomed him! I should certainly have caught him in my arms, and kissed him, if I had not been ashamed.
CAN: Wilhelm, what is the world to our hearts without love? A magic lantern without its lamp. The moment you set the little flame inside it, the brightest pictures shine out on your white wall! And even if they're nothing but passing phantoms, still it makes us happy when we stand before them like children, carried away by the wonderful sight. Today I couldn't get to Charlotte — visitors I couldn't shake off held me back. What was I to do? I sent my servant over, just to have someone near me today who'd been near her. How impatiently I waited for him, how overjoyed I was to see him back! I'd have caught him up and kissed him, if I hadn't been ashamed.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: It is said that the Bonona stone, when placed in the sun, attracts the rays, and for a time appears luminous in the dark. So was it with me and this servant. The idea that Charlotte's eyes had dwelt on his countenance, his cheek, his very apparel, endeared them all inestimably to me, so that at the moment I would not have parted from him for a thousand crowns. His presence made me so happy! Beware of laughing at me, Wilhelm. Can that be a delusion which makes us happy?
CAN: They say the Bologna stone, set out in the sun, drinks in its rays and glows for a while afterward in the dark. It was just so with me and the boy. The thought that her eyes had rested on his face, his cheeks, the buttons of his coat and the collar of his jacket, made all of it so sacred, so precious, that in that moment I wouldn't have given him up for a thousand thalers. I felt so happy in his presence. God forbid you laugh at me, Wilhelm. Can it be a delusion, if it makes us happy?

=== Ch 24: July 19, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: "I shall see her today!" I exclaim with delight, when I rise in the morning, and look out with gladness of heart at the bright, beautiful sun. "I shall see her today!" And then I have no further wish to form: all, all is included in that one thought.
CAN: "I'm going to see her!" I cry first thing in the morning, looking up cheerfully at the glorious sun. "I'm going to see her!" And then I've no other wish for the whole day. Everything, everything is swallowed up in that one prospect.

=== Ch 25: July 20, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I cannot assent to your proposal that I should accompany the ambassador to ———. I do not love subordination; and we all know that he is a rough, disagreeable person to be connected with. You say my mother wishes me to be employed. I could not help laughing at that. Am I not sufficiently employed? And is it not in reality the same, whether I shell peas or count lentils? The world runs on from one folly to another; and the man who, solely from regard to the opinion of others, and without any wish or necessity of his own, toils after gold, honour, or any other phantom, is no better than a fool.
CAN: I can't agree to your idea that I should go with the ambassador to ——. I have no love for taking orders, and besides, we all know the man is an unpleasant brute to deal with. My mother would like to see me employed, you say — that made me laugh. Am I not employed now? And what's the real difference whether I'm counting peas or lentils? Everything in the world comes down in the end to a trifle, and a man who wears himself out for the sake of others, chasing money or honors or whatever else, without a passion or a need of his own to drive him, is a fool.

=== Ch 26: July 24, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: You insist so much on my not neglecting my drawing, that it would be as well for me to say nothing as to confess how little I have lately done.
CAN: You press me so hard not to neglect my drawing that I'd rather say nothing at all than admit how little I've done lately.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: I never felt happier, I never understood nature better, even down to the veriest stem or smallest blade of grass; and yet I am unable to express myself: my powers of execution are so weak, everything seems to swim and float before me, so that I cannot make a clear, bold outline. But I fancy I should succeed better if I had some clay or wax to model. I shall try, if this state of mind continues much longer, and will take to modelling, if I only knead dough.
CAN: Never have I been happier, never have I felt nature more fully, down to the smallest pebble, the smallest blade of grass — and yet I can't find any way to put it out. My powers of execution have deserted me; everything floats and shimmers before my soul so that I can't seize a single clean outline. But I keep fancying I'd do better with clay or wax to work in. If this lasts much longer I'll get some clay and knead away, even if all that comes of it is dumplings.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: I have commenced Charlotte's portrait three times, and have as often disgraced myself. This is the more annoying, as I was formerly very happy in taking likenesses. I have since sketched her profile, and must content myself with that.
CAN: I've begun Charlotte's portrait three times now, and three times made a fool of myself — which galls me all the more because not long ago I had a real gift for taking likenesses. So in the end I just cut out her silhouette, and I'll have to make do with that.

=== Ch 27: July 25, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Yes, dear Charlotte! I will order and arrange everything. Only give me more commissions, the more the better. One thing, however, I must request: use no more writing-sand with the dear notes you send me. Today I raised your letter hastily to my lips, and it set my teeth on edge.
CAN: Yes, dear Charlotte, I'll see to everything and arrange it all; only give me more errands, the more the better. One thing, though, I must ask: no more blotting-sand on the little notes you write me. Today I raised your letter quickly to my lips, and it set my teeth grinding.

=== Ch 28: July 26, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I have often determined not to see her so frequently. But who could keep such a resolution? Every day I am exposed to the temptation, and promise faithfully that to-morrow I will really stay away: but, when tomorrow comes, I find some irresistible reason for seeing her; and, before I can account for it, I am with her again. Either she has said on the previous evening "You will be sure to call to-morrow,"—and who could stay away then?—or she gives me some commission, and I find it essential to take her the answer in person; or the day is fine, and I walk to Walheim; and, when I am there, it is only half a league farther to her. I am within the charmed atmosphere, and soon find myself at her side. My grandmother used to tell us a story of a mountain of loadstone. When any vessels came near it, they were instantly deprived of their ironwork: the nails flew to the mountain, and the unhappy crew perished amidst the disjointed planks.
CAN: Many a time I've resolved not to see her so often. But who could keep such a vow! Every day I give in to the temptation, swearing faithfully that tomorrow, for once, I'll stay away — and when tomorrow comes I find some irresistible reason all over again, and before I know how, I'm with her. Either she's said the evening before, "You'll come tomorrow, won't you?" — and who could stay away after that? — or she gives me an errand, and I find it only proper to bring her the answer myself; or the day is too fine, and I walk out to Walheim, and once I'm there it's only half a league more to her — and I'm too close to the spell not to be drawn in, and there I am with her again. My grandmother used to tell us a tale of a magnetic mountain: any ship that sailed too near it was stripped of all its iron in an instant, the nails flying off to the mountain, and the poor sailors went down among the collapsing planks.

=== Ch 29: July 30, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Albert is arrived, and I must take my departure. Were he the best and noblest of men, and I in every respect his inferior, I could not endure to see him in possession of such a perfect being. Possession!—enough, Wilhelm: her betrothed is here,—a fine, worthy fellow, whom one cannot help liking. Fortunately I was not present at their meeting. It would have broken my heart! And he is so considerate: he has not given Charlotte one kiss in my presence. Heaven reward him for it! I must love him for the respect with which he treats her. He shows a regard for me, but for this I suspect I am more indebted to Charlotte than to his own fancy for me. Women have a delicate tact in such matters, and it should be so. They cannot always succeed in keeping two rivals on terms with each other; but, when they do, they are the only gainers.
CAN: Albert has arrived, and now I must go. Were he the best, the noblest of men, a man I'd gladly put above myself in every way, I still couldn't bear to watch him possess such perfection before my eyes. Possess — enough, Wilhelm: the betrothed is here. A fine, good-hearted fellow you can't help but like. Luckily I wasn't there when they met — it would have torn my heart apart. And he's so considerate; he hasn't kissed Charlotte once in my presence. Heaven reward him for it! I have to love him for the respect he shows her. He's kindly disposed toward me, though I suspect that's Charlotte's doing more than his own feeling. Women have a fine touch in these things, and rightly so — it's all to their good if they can keep two admirers on good terms, however seldom they manage it.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: I cannot help esteeming Albert. The coolness of his temper contrasts strongly with the impetuosity of mine, which I cannot conceal. He has a great deal of feeling, and is fully sensible of the treasure he possesses in Charlotte. He is free from ill-humour, which you know is the fault I detest most.
CAN: All the same, I can't help respecting Albert. His outward calm stands in sharp relief against my own restlessness, which I can't hide. He has plenty of feeling and knows full well what a treasure he has in Charlotte. He seems free of ill-humor, and you know that's the failing I hate above all others in a man.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: He regards me as a man of sense; and my attachment to Charlotte, and the interest I take in all that concerns her, augment his triumph and his love. I shall not inquire whether he may not at times tease her with some little jealousies; as I know, that, were I in his place, I should not be entirely free from such sensations.
CAN: He takes me for a man of sense; and my devotion to Charlotte, the warm delight I take in everything she does, only swells his triumph, and he loves her the more for it. Whether he doesn't now and then needle her with a little jealousy I'll leave aside; in his place I certainly wouldn't be entirely safe from that devil myself.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: But, be that as it may, my pleasure with Charlotte is over. Call it folly or infatuation, what signifies a name? The thing speaks for itself. Before Albert came, I knew all that I know now. I knew I could make no pretensions to her, nor did I offer any, that is, as far as it was possible, in the presence of so much loveliness, not to pant for its enjoyment. And now, behold me like a silly fellow, staring with astonishment when another comes in, and deprives me of my love.
CAN: Be that as it may — my joy in being near Charlotte is gone. Shall I call it folly, or blindness? What's in a name? The thing speaks for itself! I knew everything I know now before Albert came; I knew I had no claim to make on her, and I made none — that is, so far as it's possible not to ache with longing in the presence of so much loveliness. And now the fool stands here goggling, because another man has actually turned up and is carrying the girl off.
--- para 4 ---
SRC: I bite my lips, and feel infinite scorn for those who tell me to be resigned, because there is no help for it. Let me escape from the yoke of such silly subterfuges! I ramble through the woods; and when I return to Charlotte, and find Albert sitting by her side in the summer-house in the garden, I am unable to bear it, behave like a fool, and commit a thousand extravagances. "For Heaven's sake," said Charlotte today, "let us have no more scenes like those of last night! You terrify me when you are so violent." Between ourselves, I am always away now when he visits her: and I feel delighted when I find her alone.
CAN: I grind my teeth and mock my own misery — and I'd mock twice over anyone who told me to resign myself, since nothing can be done. Spare me these straw men! I run through the woods, and when I come to Charlotte and find Albert sitting beside her in the little garden arbor, and I can't go on, I turn wild and play the fool and get up to a hundred follies. "For God's sake," Charlotte said to me today, "no more scenes like last night, I beg you! You frighten me when you're so worked up." Between us — I watch now for the hours he's away on business, and off I go, and I'm always happy when I find her alone.

=== Ch 30: August 8, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Believe me, dear Wilhelm, I did not allude to you when I spoke so severely of those who advise resignation to inevitable fate. I did not think it possible for you to indulge such a sentiment. But in fact you are right. I only suggest one objection. In this world one is seldom reduced to make a selection between two alternatives. There are as many varieties of conduct and opinion as there are turns of feature between an aquiline nose and a flat one.
CAN: Believe me, dear Wilhelm, I didn't mean you when I spoke so harshly of the people who tell us to bow to the inevitable. I truly never thought you might hold such a view. And at bottom you're right. Only one thing, my friend: in this world it's very seldom a matter of either-or. Feelings and ways of acting have as many gradations as there are shades of nose between a hooked one and a snub.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: You will, therefore, permit me to concede your entire argument, and yet contrive means to escape your dilemma.
CAN: So you'll allow me to grant your whole argument and still try to slip out between its horns.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: Your position is this, I hear you say: "Either you have hopes of obtaining Charlotte, or you have none. Well, in the first case, pursue your course, and press on to the fulfilment of your wishes. In the second, be a man, and shake off a miserable passion, which will enervate and destroy you." My dear friend, this is well and easily said.
CAN: It comes to this, you say: either you have some hope of winning Charlotte, or you have none. Good — in the first case, push on and try to bring your wish to fruit; in the second, pull yourself together and try to be rid of a wretched feeling that will eat up all your strength. My dear fellow — well said, and easily said.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: But would you require a wretched being, whose life is slowly wasting under a lingering disease, to despatch himself at once by the stroke of a dagger? Does not the very disorder which consumes his strength deprive him of the courage to effect his deliverance?
CAN: But would you ask a poor wretch whose life is being slowly gnawed away by a creeping illness to put a quick end to his torment with one stroke of the knife? Doesn't the very disease that drains his strength also rob him of the courage to free himself?
--- para 4 ---
SRC: You may answer me, if you please, with a similar analogy, "Who would not prefer the amputation of an arm to the periling of life by doubt and procrastination!" But I know not if I am right, and let us leave these comparisons.
CAN: You could answer me with a kindred analogy: who wouldn't rather have his arm taken off than risk his life by hesitating and hanging back? I don't know. But let's not trade comparisons any longer.
--- para 5 ---
SRC: Enough! There are moments, Wilhelm, when I could rise up and shake it all off, and when, if I only knew where to go, I could fly from this place.
CAN: Enough. There are moments, Wilhelm, when I could leap up and shake the whole thing off, and then, if only I knew where to, I think I would go.
--- para 6 ---
SRC: THE SAME EVENING.
CAN: That same evening.
--- para 7 ---
SRC: My diary, which I have for some time neglected, came before me today; and I am amazed to see how deliberately I have entangled myself step by step. To have seen my position so clearly, and yet to have acted so like a child! Even still I behold the result plainly, and yet have no thought of acting with greater prudence.
CAN: My diary, which I've let lie for a while, came into my hands again today, and I'm amazed how knowingly I walked into all of this, step by step. To have seen my situation so clearly the whole time, and yet to have behaved like a child — to see it even now, plainly, and still show no sign of growing any wiser.

=== Ch 31: August 10, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: If I were not a fool, I could spend the happiest and most delightful life here. So many agreeable circumstances, and of a kind to ensure a worthy man's happiness, are seldom united. Alas! I feel it too sensibly,—the heart alone makes our happiness! To be admitted into this most charming family, to be loved by the father as a son, by the children as a father, and by Charlotte! then the noble Albert, who never disturbs my happiness by any appearance of ill-humour, receiving me with the heartiest affection, and loving me, next to Charlotte, better than all the world! Wilhelm, you would be delighted to hear us in our rambles, and conversations about Charlotte. Nothing in the world can be more absurd than our connection, and yet the thought of it often moves me to tears.
CAN: I could be living the happiest, finest life here, if only I weren't a fool. Such pleasant circumstances, and so well suited to delight a man's soul, rarely come together. Ah — how true it is that the heart alone makes our happiness. To be part of this lovely family; loved by the old man like a son, by the little ones like a father, and by Charlotte — and then the good Albert, who never spoils my happiness with any sour mood, but takes me in with the warmest friendship, and after Charlotte loves me best in the world! Wilhelm, it's a joy to hear the two of us on our walks, talking about Charlotte. Nothing more absurd than this whole arrangement was ever invented in the world, and yet it often brings tears to my eyes.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: He tells me sometimes of her excellent mother; how, upon her death-bed, she had committed her house and children to Charlotte, and had given Charlotte herself in charge to him; how, since that time, a new spirit had taken possession of her; how, in care and anxiety for their welfare, she became a real mother to them; how every moment of her time was devoted to some labour of love in their behalf,—and yet her mirth and cheerfulness had never forsaken her. I walk by his side, pluck flowers by the way, arrange them carefully into a nosegay, then fling them into the first stream I pass, and watch them as they float gently away. I forget whether I told you that Albert is to remain here. He has received a government appointment, with a very good salary; and I understand he is in high favour at court. I have met few persons so punctual and methodical in business.
CAN: He tells me about her good mother — how on her deathbed she handed over her house and children to Charlotte, and Charlotte herself to him; how from that day a new spirit had filled the girl, how in her care and worry over the household she became a true mother to them, how not one moment of her time passed without active love and work, and yet her cheerfulness and her light heart never left her. I walk along beside him, picking flowers by the path, weaving them carefully into a little wreath, and then — throwing it into the stream as it runs by, watching it bob gently away downstream. I don't know whether I told you that Albert is to stay here; he's been given a post at court with a good salary, and stands high in favor there. In matters of business I've met few people so orderly and exact.

=== Ch 32: August 12, 1771 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Certainly Albert is the best fellow in the world. I had a strange scene with him yesterday. I went to take leave of him; for I took it into my head to spend a few days in these mountains, from where I now write to you. As I was walking up and down his room, my eye fell upon his pistols. "Lend me those pistols," said I, "for my journey." "By all means," he replied, "if you will take the trouble to load them; for they only hang there for form." I took down one of them; and he continued, "Ever since I was near suffering for my extreme caution, I will have nothing to do with such things." I was curious to hear the story. "I was staying," said he, "some three months ago, at a friend's house in the country. I had a brace of pistols with me, unloaded; and I slept without any anxiety. One rainy afternoon I was sitting by myself, doing nothing, when it occurred to me I do not know how that the house might be attacked, that we might require the pistols, that we might in short, you know how we go on fancying, when we have nothing better to do. I gave the pistols to the servant, to clean and load. He was playing with the maid, and trying to frighten her, when the pistol went off—God knows how!—the ramrod was in the barrel; and it went straight through her right hand, and shattered the thumb. I had to endure all the lamentation, and to pay the surgeon's bill; so, since that time, I have kept all my weapons unloaded. But, my dear friend, what is the use of prudence? We can never be on our guard against all possible dangers. However,"—now, you must know I can tolerate all men till they come to "however;"—for it is self-evident that every universal rule must have its exceptions. But he is so exceedingly accurate, that, if he only fancies he has said a word too precipitate, or too general, or only half true, he never ceases to qualify, to modify, and extenuate, till at last he appears to have said nothing at all. Upon this occasion, Albert was deeply immersed in his subject: I ceased to listen to him, and became lost in reverie. With a sudden motion, I pointed the mouth of the pistol to my forehead, over the right eye. "What do you mean?" cried Albert, turning back the pistol. "It is not loaded," said I. "And even if not," he answered with impatience, "what can you mean? I cannot comprehend how a man can be so mad as to shoot himself, and the bare idea of it shocks me."
CAN: Albert is certainly the best fellow alive. I had a strange scene with him yesterday. I'd gone to take my leave, since the whim took me to ride up into these hills, from which I'm writing to you now. As I was pacing his room my eye fell on his pistols. "Lend me the pistols," I said, "for the journey." "Gladly," he said, "if you'll take the trouble to load them; they only hang there for show." I took one down, and he went on, "Ever since my caution played me such a nasty trick, I won't have anything more to do with the things." I was curious to hear the story. "I was staying about three months," he said, "at a friend's place in the country. I had a pair of unloaded pocket-pistols, and slept easy. One rainy afternoon, sitting about with nothing to do, it came into my head — I don't know how — that we might be attacked, that we might need the pistols, that — well, you know how it goes when one has nothing better to do. I handed them to the servant to clean and load. He was fooling with the maid, trying to scare her, and somehow — God knows how — the gun went off, the ramrod still down the barrel, and drove it into the ball of her right thumb and smashed it. So I had her wailing to put up with and the surgeon's bill to pay into the bargain, and ever since I've kept my weapons unloaded. My dear fellow, what good is caution? You can't learn every danger in advance. To be sure —" Now, you must know, I can get along with a man right up to his "to be sure"; for surely it goes without saying that every general rule has its exceptions. But the man is so terribly careful that the moment he thinks he's said something a bit too rash, too sweeping, only half true, he won't stop qualifying and trimming and whittling away until in the end there's nothing left of it at all. And on this occasion he got so deep into the text that I stopped listening to him altogether and fell to brooding, and with one sudden motion I pressed the muzzle of the pistol to my forehead, above the right eye. "Good God," cried Albert, pulling the pistol down, "what is this?" "It isn't loaded," I said. "Even so — what do you mean by it?" he snapped. "I can't imagine how a man could be foolish enough to shoot himself; the very thought disgusts me."
--- para 1 ---
SRC: "But why should any one," said I, "in speaking of an action, venture to pronounce it mad or wise, or good or bad? What is the meaning of all this? Have you carefully studied the secret motives of our actions? Do you understand—can you explain the causes which occasion them, and make them inevitable? If you can, you will be less hasty with your decision."
CAN: "But why is it," I said, "that whenever you people talk of some action, you have to come straight out with: this is foolish, that is clever, this is good, that is bad? And what does any of it mean? Have you really looked into the inner workings of an act? Can you account for the reasons, the necessity, that made it happen and had to make it happen? If you had, you wouldn't be so quick with your verdicts."
--- para 2 ---
SRC: "But you will allow," said Albert; "that some actions are criminal, let them spring from whatever motives they may." I granted it, and shrugged my shoulders.
CAN: "But you'll grant me," said Albert, "that certain acts are wicked, whatever the motive behind them." I shrugged and let it pass.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: "But still, my good friend," I continued, "there are some exceptions here too. Theft is a crime; but the man who commits it from extreme poverty, with no design but to save his family from perishing, is he an object of pity, or of punishment? Who shall throw the first stone at a husband, who, in the heat of just resentment, sacrifices his faithless wife and her perfidious seducer? or at the young maiden, who, in her weak hour of rapture, forgets herself in the impetuous joys of love? Even our laws, cold and cruel as they are, relent in such cases, and withhold their punishment."
CAN: "Still, my dear fellow," I went on, "even here there are exceptions. It's true that theft is a crime; but a man who steals to save himself and his family from starving — does he deserve pity or punishment? Who casts the first stone at the husband who in righteous fury kills his unfaithful wife and her worthless seducer? at the girl who, in one rapturous hour, loses herself in the irresistible joys of love? Even our laws, cold and pedantic as they are, let themselves be moved and hold back their punishment."
--- para 4 ---
SRC: "That is quite another thing," said Albert; "because a man under the influence of violent passion loses all power of reflection, and is regarded as intoxicated or insane."
CAN: "That's a different matter entirely," said Albert, "because a man swept away by his passions loses all power of reflection and counts as a drunkard or a madman."
--- para 5 ---
SRC: "Oh! you people of sound understandings," I replied, smiling, "are ever ready to exclaim 'Extravagance, and madness, and intoxication!' You moral men are so calm and so subdued! You abhor the drunken man, and detest the extravagant; you pass by, like the Levite, and thank God, like the Pharisee, that you are not like one of them. I have been more than once intoxicated, my passions have always bordered on extravagance: I am not ashamed to confess it; for I have learned, by my own experience, that all extraordinary men, who have accomplished great and astonishing actions, have ever been decried by the world as drunken or insane. And in private life, too, is it not intolerable that no one can undertake the execution of a noble or generous deed, without giving rise to the exclamation that the doer is intoxicated or mad? Shame upon you, ye sages!"
CAN: "Oh, you reasonable people!" I cried, smiling. "Passion! Drunkenness! Madness! There you stand, so cool, so unconcerned, you moral men — scolding the drunkard, loathing the madman, passing by on the other side like the Levite, and thanking God like the Pharisee that he hasn't made you as one of them. I've been drunk more than once, my passions have never been far from madness, and I'm not ashamed of either; for I've learned, in my own way, that all extraordinary men who ever did something great, something that seemed impossible, have always been cried down as drunk or mad. And in ordinary life too it's unbearable to hear it said of nearly anyone who does a halfway free, noble, unexpected thing: the man is drunk, he's a fool! Shame on you sober ones, shame on you sages!"
--- para 6 ---
SRC: "This is another of your extravagant humours," said Albert: "you always exaggerate a case, and in this matter you are undoubtedly wrong; for we were speaking of suicide, which you compare with great actions, when it is impossible to regard it as anything but a weakness. It is much easier to die than to bear a life of misery with fortitude."
CAN: "There you go again with your fancies," said Albert; "you overstrain everything, and here at least you're plainly wrong to set suicide, which is what we're talking about, beside great deeds — when it can't be seen as anything but a weakness. After all, it's easier to die than to bear a life of torment with backbone."
--- para 7 ---
SRC: I was on the point of breaking off the conversation, for nothing puts me so completely out of patience as the utterance of a wretched commonplace when I am talking from my inmost heart. However, I composed myself, for I had often heard the same observation with sufficient vexation; and I answered him, therefore, with a little warmth, "You call this a weakness—beware of being led astray by appearances. When a nation, which has long groaned under the intolerable yoke of a tyrant, rises at last and throws off its chains, do you call that weakness? The man who, to rescue his house from the flames, finds his physical strength redoubled, so that he lifts burdens with ease, which, in the absence of excitement, he could scarcely move; he who, under the rage of an insult, attacks and puts to flight half a score of his enemies, are such persons to be called weak? My good friend, if resistance be strength, how can the highest degree of resistance be a weakness?"
CAN: I was on the point of breaking it off, for nothing throws me off balance like having someone trot out an empty platitude while I'm speaking from the bottom of my heart. But I checked myself, since I'd heard it often enough and been vexed by it often enough, and answered him rather hotly: "You call it weakness? I beg you, don't be fooled by appearances. When a people groaning under a tyrant's unbearable yoke at last rises up and breaks its chains, will you call that weakness? And the man who, with his house in flames, feels his strength so doubled by terror that he easily carries off loads he could hardly stir when calm; the man who in the fury of an insult takes on six others and overpowers them — are these to be called weak? My good friend, if straining every nerve is strength, why should straining it to the utmost be the opposite?"
--- para 8 ---
