I was engaged in observing Miss B—, and did not notice that the women were whispering at the end of the room, that the murmur extended by degrees to the men, that Madame S— addressed the count with much warmth (this was all related to me subsequently by Miss B—); till at length the count came up to me, and took me to the window. "You know our ridiculous customs," he said. "I perceive the company is rather displeased at your being here. I would not on any account—" "I beg your excellency's pardon!" I exclaimed. "I ought to have thought of this before, but I know you will forgive this little inattention. I was going," I added, "some time ago, but my evil genius detained me." And I smiled and bowed, to take my leave. He shook me by the hand, in a manner which expressed everything. I hastened at once from the illustrious assembly, sprang into a carriage, and drove to M—. I contemplated the setting sun from the top of the hill, and read that beautiful passage in Homer, where Ulysses is entertained by the hospitable herdsmen. This was indeed delightful.
CAN: Count O— likes me, singles me out — you know that, I've told you a hundred times. Yesterday I dined with him, on the very day his grand company of lords and ladies gathers at his house in the evening, which I never gave a thought to, and it never struck me that we underlings don't belong there. Good. I dine with the count, and after dinner we walk up and down the great hall; I talk with him, with Colonel B— who joins us, and so the hour for the party comes on. God knows, I'm thinking of nothing. In sweeps the more-than-gracious Lady von S— with her lord and her well-hatched little gosling of a daughter, with her flat chest and trim little waist; in passing they make their high-born eyes and nostrils at me in the old approved style. And since I heartily detest the whole tribe, I was just about to take my leave, only waiting for the count to be free of their wretched chatter, when my Miss B— came in. As my heart always lifts a little at the sight of her, I stayed on, stood behind her chair, and only after a while noticed that she was talking to me with less than her usual openness, a little flustered. It struck me. Is she like all the rest? I thought, and was stung, and meant to go — and yet stayed, because I wanted to excuse her, and didn't believe it, and still hoped for a kind word from her, and — whatever you like. Meanwhile the room filled up. Baron F— in a whole wardrobe inherited from the coronation of Francis I; the court councillor R—, with his deaf wife; the shabbily turned-out J—, patching the gaps of his old-fashioned coat with new bits. They came in droves, and I talked with a few of my acquaintances, who were all very curt. I was thinking — and only had eyes for my Miss B—. I didn't notice the women whispering at the far end of the room, that it spread to the men, that Frau von S— spoke to the count (Miss B— told me all this afterward), until at last the count came up to me and drew me over to a window. "You know our odd ways," he said. "The company, I notice, is displeased to see you here. I wouldn't for the world —" "Your Excellency," I broke in, "I beg your pardon a thousand times; I should have thought of it sooner, and I know you'll forgive me this inconsistency. I meant to take my leave a while ago; an evil genius held me back," I added with a smile, and bowed. The count pressed my hand with a feeling that said everything. I slipped quietly out of the grand company, got into a carriage, and drove to M—, to watch the sunset from the hill there and read in my Homer the glorious canto where Ulysses is entertained by the noble swineherd. That was good.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: I returned home to supper in the evening. But few persons were assembled in the room. They had turned up a corner of the table-cloth, and were playing at dice. The good-natured A— came in. He laid down his hat when he saw me, approached me, and said in a low tone, "You have met with a disagreeable adventure." "I!" I exclaimed. "The count obliged you to withdraw from the assembly!" "Deuce take the assembly!" said I. "I was very glad to be gone." "I am delighted," he added, "that you take it so lightly. I am only sorry that it is already so much spoken of." The circumstance then began to pain me. I fancied that every one who sat down, and even looked at me, was thinking of this incident; and my heart became embittered.
CAN: In the evening I came back to supper. Only a few were still in the room, dicing in a corner with the cloth turned back. Then the good A— came in, laid down his hat when he saw me, came up to me and said softly, "You've had trouble?" "I?" I said. "The count sent you out of the party." "The devil take the party," I said. "I was glad to get into the open air." "Good," he said, "that you take it lightly. Only it vexes me that it's already all over the place." Now the thing began to gnaw at me. Everyone who came to table and looked at me — I imagined it was because of that. It made my blood boil.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: And now I could plunge a dagger into my bosom, when I hear myself everywhere pitied, and observe the triumph of my enemies, who say that this is always the case with vain persons, whose heads are turned with conceit, who affect to despise forms and such petty, idle nonsense.
CAN: And now, when wherever I go I'm pitied, when I hear my enviers crowing — There, you see what comes of conceited heads who plume themselves on their little wit and think they may flout every convention! — and more such kennel-talk — then I could drive a knife into my heart.
--- para 4 ---
SRC: Say what you will of fortitude, but show me the man who can patiently endure the laughter of fools, when they have obtained an advantage over him. 'Tis only when their nonsense is without foundation that one can suffer it without complaint.
CAN: Say what you will about a man standing on his own feet — show me the one who can bear to have scoundrels run him down when they've got the better of him. When their gossip is empty, ah, then you can let it pass.
--- para 5 ---
SRC: March 16.
CAN: March 16.
--- para 6 ---
SRC: Everything conspires against me. I met Miss B— walking to-day. I could not help joining her; and, when we were at a little distance from her companions, I expressed my sense of her altered manner toward me. "O Werther!" she said, in a tone of emotion, "you, who know my heart, how could you so ill interpret my distress? What did I not suffer for you, from the moment you entered the room! I foresaw it all, a hundred times was I on the point of mentioning it to you. I knew that the S——s and T——s, with their husbands, would quit the room, rather than remain in your company. I knew that the count would not break with them: and now so much is said about it." "How!" I exclaimed, and endeavoured to conceal my emotion; for all that Adelin had mentioned to me yesterday recurred to me painfully at that moment. "Oh, how much it has already cost me!" said this amiable girl, while her eyes filled with tears. I could scarcely contain myself, and was ready to throw myself at her feet. "Explain yourself!" I cried. Tears flowed down her cheeks. I became quite frantic. She wiped them away, without attempting to conceal them. "You know my aunt," she continued; "she was present: and in what light does she consider the affair! Last night, and this morning, Werther, I was compelled to listen to a lecture upon my acquaintance with you. I have been obliged to hear you condemned and depreciated; and I could not—I dared not—say much in your defence."
CAN: Everything's after me. Today I met Miss B— out walking; I couldn't help speaking to her, and as soon as we were a little away from the others I told her how hurt I'd been by her manner the other day. "Oh, Werther," she said warmly, "how could you read my confusion that way, you who know my heart? What I suffered for you, from the moment I stepped into the room! I saw it all coming; a hundred times it was on the tip of my tongue to warn you. I knew the von S—s and the von T—s would sooner leave with their husbands than stay in your company; I knew the count couldn't afford to break with them — and now this fuss!" "What do you mean?" I said, hiding my fright — for everything Adelin had told me the day before ran like boiling water through my veins in that instant. "What it has already cost me!" said the sweet creature, with tears in her eyes. I was no longer master of myself, ready to throw myself at her feet. "Tell me what you mean!" I cried. The tears ran down her cheeks; I was beside myself. She wiped them away without trying to hide them. "You know my aunt," she began. "She was there and saw it all — with what eyes, Werther! Last night, and this morning, I've had to hear a sermon about my acquaintance with you, sit there and listen to you belittled and run down, and could not — dared not — defend you fully."
--- para 7 ---
SRC: Every word she uttered was a dagger to my heart. She did not feel what a mercy it would have been to conceal everything from me. She told me, in addition, all the impertinence that would be further circulated, and how the malicious would triumph; how they would rejoice over the punishment of my pride, over my humiliation for that want of esteem for others with which I had often been reproached. To hear all this, Wilhelm, uttered by her in a voice of the most sincere sympathy, awakened all my passions; and I am still in a state of extreme excitement. I wish I could find a man to jeer me about this event. I would sacrifice him to my resentment. The sight of his blood might possibly be a relief to my fury. A hundred times have I seized a dagger, to give ease to this oppressed heart. Naturalists tell of a noble race of horses that instinctively open a vein with their teeth, when heated and exhausted by a long course, in order to breathe more freely. I am often tempted to open a vein, to procure for myself everlasting liberty.
CAN: Every word she spoke went through my heart like a sword. She never felt what a mercy it would have been to keep it all from me; and on top of it she told me what else would be said about it, how the spiteful would gloat. How they'd revel in my comeuppance, my humbling, for the pride and contempt of others they've so often thrown in my face. To hear all that, Wilhelm, from her, in a voice of the truest sympathy — I was shattered, and I'm still raging inside. I wish someone would dare to throw it in my face, so I could run him through the body; the sight of blood would do me good. Oh, a hundred times I've taken up a knife to give my choked heart air. They tell of a noble breed of horses that, when they're driven too hard and run hot, bite open one of their own veins by instinct, to breathe. I often feel the same: I'd like to open a vein and win myself everlasting freedom.

=== Ch 49: March 24, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I have tendered my resignation to the court. I hope it will be accepted, and you will forgive me for not having previously consulted you. It is necessary I should leave this place. I know all you will urge me to stay, and therefore I beg you will soften this news to my mother. I am unable to do anything for myself: how, then, should I be competent to assist others? It will afflict her that I should have interrupted that career which would have made me first a privy councillor, and then minister, and that I should look behind me, in place of advancing. Argue as you will, combine all the reasons which should have induced me to remain, I am going: that is sufficient. But, that you may not be ignorant of my destination, I may mention that the Prince of —— is here. He is much pleased with my company; and, having heard of my intention to resign, he has invited me to his country house, to pass the spring months with him. I shall be left completely my own master; and, as we agree on all subjects but one, I shall try my fortune, and accompany him.
CAN: I've sent in my resignation to the court, and I hope it will be accepted; forgive me for not asking your leave first. I have to get away, and I know all you'll say to talk me into staying, so — sweeten it for my mother. I can't help myself, so she'll have to put up with my not being able to help her either. It will hurt her, of course, to see the fine career her son had begun — privy councillor, ambassador, and who knows what — pulled up short, and the little horse stabled again. Make of it what you will, reckon up all the ways and means by which I might have stayed: enough, I'm going. But so you'll know where I'm bound — there's a Prince of —— here who has taken a great liking to my company; when he heard of my plans he asked me to come with him to his estates and spend the lovely spring there. I'm to be left entirely to myself, he promises, and since the two of us understand each other up to a point, I'll chance it and go with him.

=== Ch 50: April 19, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Thanks for both your letters. I delayed my reply, and withheld this letter, till I should obtain an answer from the court. I feared my mother might apply to the minister to defeat my purpose. But my request is granted, my resignation is accepted. I shall not recount with what reluctance it was accorded, nor relate what the minister has written: you would only renew your lamentations. The crown prince has sent me a present of five and twenty ducats; and, indeed, such goodness has affected me to tears. For this reason I shall not require from my mother the money for which I lately applied.
CAN: Thank you for both your letters. I didn't answer because I left this sheet lying until my discharge should come through from the court; I was afraid my mother might turn to the minister and make my plan harder. But now it's done, my discharge has come. I won't tell you how reluctantly they granted it, nor what the minister wrote — you'd only start your lamenting again. The crown prince has sent me a parting gift of twenty-five ducats, with a word that moved me to tears; so I won't need the money from my mother that I asked for lately.

=== Ch 51: May 5, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I leave this place to-morrow; and, as my native place is only six miles from the high road, I intend to visit it once more, and recall the happy dreams of my childhood. I shall enter at the same gate through which I came with my mother, when, after my father's death, she left that delightful retreat to immure herself in your melancholy town. Adieu, my dear friend: you shall hear of my future career.
CAN: I leave here tomorrow, and since my birthplace lies only six miles off the road, I want to see it again, to remember the old days and their dreams. I'll go in by the same gate my mother drove out of with me when, after my father's death, she left the dear, familiar place to shut herself up in her unbearable town. Adieu, Wilhelm; you'll hear how the journey goes.

=== Ch 52: May 9, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I have paid my visit to my native place with all the devotion of a pilgrim, and have experienced many unexpected emotions. Near the great elm tree, which is a quarter of a league from the village, I got out of the carriage, and sent it on before, that alone, and on foot, I might enjoy vividly and heartily all the pleasure of my recollections. I stood there under that same elm which was formerly the term and object of my walks. How things have since changed! Then, in happy ignorance, I sighed for a world I did not know, where I hoped to find every pleasure and enjoyment which my heart could desire; and now, on my return from that wide world, O my friend, how many disappointed hopes and unsuccessful plans have I brought back!
CAN: I've made the pilgrimage to my birthplace with all a pilgrim's reverence, and many an unlooked-for feeling took hold of me. By the great linden that stands a quarter-hour from the town on the way to S—, I had the carriage stop, got down, and sent it on, so as to go on foot and taste every memory new and warm and to my heart's content. There I stood under the linden that was once, when I was a boy, the goal and the boundary of my walks. How different now! Then, in happy ignorance, I longed out into the unknown world, where I hoped to find such nourishment, such delight for my heart, to fill and satisfy its stirring, hungry yearning. Now I come back from that wide world — oh, my friend, with how many shattered hopes, how many ruined plans!
--- para 1 ---
SRC: As I contemplated the mountains which lay stretched out before me, I thought how often they had been the object of my dearest desires. Here used I to sit for hours together with my eyes bent upon them, ardently longing to wander in the shade of those woods, to lose myself in those valleys, which form so delightful an object in the distance. With what reluctance did I leave this charming spot; when my hour of recreation was over, and my leave of absence expired! I drew near to the village: all the well-known old summerhouses and gardens were recognised again; I disliked the new ones, and all other alterations which had taken place. I entered the village, and all my former feelings returned. I cannot, my dear friend, enter into details, charming as were my sensations: they would be dull in the narration. I had intended to lodge in the market-place, near our old house. As soon as I entered, I perceived that the schoolroom, where our childhood had been taught by that good old woman, was converted into a shop. I called to mind the sorrow, the heaviness, the tears, and oppression of heart, which I experienced in that confinement. Every step produced some particular impression. A pilgrim in the Holy Land does not meet so many spots pregnant with tender recollections, and his soul is hardly moved with greater devotion. One incident will serve for illustration. I followed the course of a stream to a farm, formerly a delightful walk of mine, and paused at the spot, where, when boys, we used to amuse ourselves making ducks and drakes upon the water. I recollected so well how I used formerly to watch the course of that same stream, following it with inquiring eagerness, forming romantic ideas of the countries it was to pass through; but my imagination was soon exhausted: while the water continued flowing farther and farther on, till my fancy became bewildered by the contemplation of an invisible distance. Exactly such, my dear friend, so happy and so confined, were the thoughts of our good ancestors. Their feelings and their poetry were fresh as childhood. And, when Ulysses talks of the immeasurable sea and boundless earth, his epithets are true, natural, deeply felt, and mysterious. Of what importance is it that I have learned, with every schoolboy, that the world is round? Man needs but little earth for enjoyment, and still less for his final repose.
CAN: I saw the mountains lying before me that had been the goal of my longing a thousand times. I used to sit here for hours, straining toward them, losing myself heart and soul in the woods and valleys that lay there so sweetly, half-hid, before my fond eyes — and then, when the hour came to turn back, with what reluctance I left this dear spot! I came up to the town; I greeted all the old, familiar little garden houses, and disliked the new ones, and all the other changes that had been made. I went in through the gate, and at once knew myself again completely. Dear friend, I won't go into the details; charming as it all was to me, it would grow dull in the telling. I had decided to lodge in the marketplace, right beside our old house. On my way I noticed that the schoolroom where a good old woman had penned up our childhood has been turned into a shop. I remembered the restlessness, the tears, the dullness of mind, the cramped heart, I suffered in that hole. I couldn't take a step that wasn't remarkable. A pilgrim in the Holy Land doesn't pass so many spots of sacred memory, and his soul is hardly fuller of holy stirrings. One more thing, to stand for a thousand: I went down along the river to a certain farmhouse — that used to be my walk too — to the little places where we boys practiced making the flattest stones skip the most times across the water. I remembered so vividly how I'd sometimes stand and watch the water, with what wondering presentiments I followed it, how marvelously I pictured the regions it was flowing to, and how soon I found the limits of my imagining — and yet it had to go on, and on, until I lost myself altogether in the sight of an invisible distance. There, my friend, just so hemmed in and just so happy were our glorious forefathers; so childlike their feeling, their poetry. When Ulysses speaks of the immeasurable sea and the boundless earth, it's so true, so human, so heartfelt, so narrow and mysterious. What good does it do me now that I can repeat with any schoolboy that the earth is round? A man needs only a few clods to be happy on, and fewer still to lie under.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: I am at present with the prince at his hunting lodge. He is a man with whom one can live happily. He is honest and unaffected. There are, however, some strange characters about him, whom I cannot at all understand. They do not seem vicious, and yet they do not carry the appearance of thoroughly honest men. Sometimes I am disposed to believe them honest, and yet I cannot persuade myself to confide in them. It grieves me to hear the prince occasionally talk of things which he has only read or heard of, and always with the same view in which they have been represented by others.
CAN: I'm at the prince's hunting lodge now. He's a man you can live with easily, open and plain. Only there are strange creatures around him whom I can't make out at all. They don't seem to be rogues, and yet they haven't the look of honest men either. Sometimes they seem honest to me, and still I can't trust them. What grieves me is to hear him talk of things he's only read or heard about, and always from the same angle some other man has shown them to him.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: He values my understanding and talents more highly than my heart, but I am proud of the latter only. It is the sole source of everything of our strength, happiness, and misery. All the knowledge I possess every one else can acquire, but my heart is exclusively my own.
CAN: And he values my mind and my talents more than this heart of mine, which after all is my one pride, the sole source of everything — all strength, all happiness, all misery. Ah, what I know, anyone can know; my heart is mine alone.

=== Ch 53: May 25, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I have had a plan in my head of which I did not intend to speak to you until it was accomplished: now that it has failed, I may as well mention it. I wished to enter the army, and had long been desirous of taking the step. This, indeed, was the chief reason for my coming here with the prince, as he is a general in the service. I communicated my design to him during one of our walks together. He disapproved of it, and it would have been actual madness not to have listened to his reasons.
CAN: I had something in my head I didn't mean to tell you about until it was done; now that nothing's come of it, it's all one. I wanted to go to the wars; I've had it at heart a long time. That, above all, was why I followed the prince here — he's a general in ——'s service. On one of our walks I let my plan slip; he advised against it, and I'd have had to be more stubborn than sensible not to give way to his reasons.

=== Ch 54: June 11, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Say what you will, I can remain here no longer. Why should I remain? Time hangs heavy upon my hands. The prince is as gracious to me as any one could be, and yet I am not at my ease. There is, indeed, nothing in common between us. He is a man of understanding, but quite of the ordinary kind. His conversation affords me no more amusement than I should derive from the perusal of a well-written book. I shall remain here a week longer, and then start again on my travels. My drawings are the best things I have done since I came here. The prince has a taste for the arts, and would improve if his mind were not fettered by cold rules and mere technical ideas. I often lose patience, when, with a glowing imagination, I am giving expression to art and nature, he interferes with learned suggestions, and uses at random the technical phraseology of artists.
CAN: Say what you like, I can't stay here any longer. What's the use? Time drags on me. The prince treats me as well as anyone could, and yet I'm not in my own element. At bottom we have nothing in common. He's a man of understanding, but of the most ordinary kind; his company gives me no more than reading a well-written book. I'll stay another week, and then off into the blue again. The best thing I've done here is my drawing. The prince has a feeling for art, and would feel it more keenly if he weren't fenced in by his wretched scholarship and the usual jargon. Many a time I grind my teeth when, with a warm imagination, I lead him through nature and art, and he, thinking to do wonderfully well, suddenly spoils it all by blundering in with some stock art-term.

=== Ch 55: July 16, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Once more I am a wanderer, a pilgrim, through the world. But what else are you!
CAN: Once more I'm a wanderer on the earth, a pilgrim. And what else are you all!

=== Ch 56: July 18, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Whither am I going? I will tell you in confidence. I am obliged to continue a fortnight longer here, and then I think it would be better for me to visit the mines in—. But I am only deluding myself thus. The fact is, I wish to be near Charlotte again, that is all. I smile at the suggestions of my heart, and obey its dictates.
CAN: Where am I bound? I'll tell you in confidence. I have to stay here another two weeks, and then I've persuaded myself I want to visit the mines in ——. But really there's nothing in it; I only want to be near Charlotte again, that's all. And I laugh at my own heart — and do as it bids.

=== Ch 57: July 29, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: No, no! it is yet well all is well! I her husband! O God, who gave me being, if thou hadst destined this happiness for me, my whole life would have been one continual thanksgiving! But I will not murmur—forgive these tears, forgive these fruitless wishes. She—my wife! Oh, the very thought of folding that dearest of Heaven's creatures in my arms! Dear Wilhelm, my whole frame feels convulsed when I see Albert put his arms around her slender waist!
CAN: No, it's well! All is well! I — her husband! O God who made me, had you kept that happiness for me, my whole life would be one long prayer. I won't quarrel with you — forgive me these tears, forgive me these vain wishes. She, my wife! To have folded the dearest creature under the sun in my arms — A shudder runs through me, Wilhelm, from head to foot, when Albert puts his arm around her slim waist.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: And shall I avow it? Why should I not, Wilhelm? She would have been happier with me than with him. Albert is not the man to satisfy the wishes of such a heart. He wants a certain sensibility; he wants—in short, their hearts do not beat in unison. How often, my dear friend, I'm reading a passage from some interesting book, when my heart and Charlotte's seemed to meet, and in a hundred other instances when our sentiments were unfolded by the story of some fictitious character, have I felt that we were made for each other! But, dear Wilhelm, he loves her with his whole soul; and what does not such a love deserve?
CAN: And — may I say it? Why not, Wilhelm? She would have been happier with me than with him! Oh, he's not the man to fill every wish of that heart. A certain want of feeling, a want — take it as you will — his heart doesn't beat in time with hers at — oh! — at some passage in a favorite book, where my heart and Charlotte's meet as one, and in a hundred other moments when it comes out, how we feel about something a third person has done. Dear Wilhelm! — true, he loves her with his whole soul, and what doesn't a love like that deserve!
--- para 2 ---
SRC: I have been interrupted by an insufferable visit. I have dried my tears, and composed my thoughts. Adieu, my best friend!
CAN: An unbearable visitor has broken in on me. I've dried my tears. My thoughts have scattered. Adieu, dear friend!

=== Ch 58: August 4, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I am not alone unfortunate. All men are disappointed in their hopes, and deceived in their expectations. I have paid a visit to my good old woman under the lime-trees. The eldest boy ran out to meet me: his exclamation of joy brought out his mother, but she had a very melancholy look. Her first word was, "Alas! dear sir, my little John is dead." He was the youngest of her children. I was silent. "And my husband has returned from Switzerland without any money; and, if some kind people had not assisted him, he must have begged his way home. He was taken ill with fever on his journey." I could answer nothing, but made the little one a present. She invited me to take some fruit: I complied, and left the place with a sorrowful heart.
CAN: I'm not the only one it goes so with. All men are cheated in their hopes, deceived in their expectations. I went to see my good woman under the lindens. The eldest boy ran to meet me; his shout of joy brought out his mother, and she looked very downcast. Her first word was: "Good sir, my little Hans is gone, alas!" — the youngest of her boys. I said nothing. "And my husband," she said, "is back from Switzerland with nothing; if some good people hadn't helped him, he'd have had to beg his way home. He caught a fever on the road." I could say nothing, and gave the little one something; she asked me to take a few apples, which I did, and left the place with a heavy heart.

=== Ch 59: August 21, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: My sensations are constantly changing. Sometimes a happy prospect opens before me; but alas! it is only for a moment; and then, when I am lost in reverie, I cannot help saying to myself, "If Albert were to die?—Yes, she would become—and I should be"—and so I pursue a chimera, till it leads me to the edge of a precipice at which I shudder.
CAN: My feelings shift from hour to hour. Sometimes a glad glimpse of life wants to open before me again — alas, only for a moment! When I lose myself this way in dreams, I can't help thinking: what if Albert were to die? Then you would — yes, she would — and then I chase the will-o'-the-wisp until it leads me to abysses I shrink back from in horror.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: When I pass through the same gate, and walk along the same road which first conducted me to Charlotte, my heart sinks within me at the change that has since taken place. All, all, is altered! No sentiment, no pulsation of my heart, is the same. My sensations are such as would occur to some departed prince whose spirit should return to visit the superb palace which he had built in happy times, adorned with costly magnificence, and left to a beloved son, but whose glory he should find departed, and its halls deserted and in ruins.
CAN: When I go out through the gate, along the road I first drove out by to fetch Charlotte for the ball, how wholly other it all is! Everything, everything is gone by. Not a hint of the world that was, not one pulse of my old feeling. I feel like a ghost coming back to the burnt-out, ruined castle that he built as a flourishing prince, fitting it out with every splendor, and left, full of hope, to a beloved son.

=== Ch 60: September 3, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I sometimes cannot understand how she can love another, how she dares love another, when I love nothing in this world so completely, so devotedly, as I love her, when I know only her, and have no other possession.
CAN: Sometimes I can't grasp it — that another can love her, may love her, when I love her so wholly, so deeply, so utterly, and know nothing, have nothing, am nothing but her!

=== Ch 61: September 4, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: It is even so! As nature puts on her autumn tints it becomes autumn with me and around me. My leaves are sere and yellow, and the neighbouring trees are divested of their foliage. Do you remember my writing to you about a peasant boy shortly after my arrival here? I have just made inquiries about him in Walheim. They say he has been dismissed from his service, and is now avoided by every one. I met him yesterday on the road, going to a neighbouring village. I spoke to him, and he told me his story. It interested me exceedingly, as you will easily understand when I repeat it to you. But why should I trouble you? Why should I not reserve all my sorrow for myself? Why should I continue to give you occasion to pity and blame me? But no matter: this also is part of my destiny.
CAN: Yes, so it is. As nature tips toward autumn, autumn comes in me and around me. My leaves are yellowing, and the leaves of the trees nearby have already fallen. Didn't I write to you, when I first came here, about a peasant lad? I asked after him again now in Walheim; they said he'd been turned out of his place, and no one would tell me more. Yesterday I met him on the road to another village; I spoke to him, and he told me his story, which moved me doubly and triply, as you'll easily understand when I tell it to you. But what's the use — why don't I keep what frightens and hurts me to myself? Why do I sadden you too? Why do I keep giving you cause to pity me and scold me? No matter — that too belongs to my fate.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: At first the peasant lad answered my inquiries with a sort of subdued melancholy, which seemed to me the mark of a timid disposition; but, as we grew to understand each other, he spoke with less reserve, and openly confessed his faults, and lamented his misfortune. I wish, my dear friend, I could give proper expression to his language. He told me with a sort of pleasurable recollection, that, after my departure, his passion for his mistress increased daily, until at last he neither knew what he did nor what he said, nor what was to become of him. He could neither eat nor drink nor sleep: he felt a sense of suffocation; he disobeyed all orders, and forgot all commands involuntarily; he seemed as if pursued by an evil spirit, till one day, knowing that his mistress had gone to an upper chamber, he had followed, or, rather, been drawn after her. As she proved deaf to his entreaties, he had recourse to violence. He knows not what happened; but he called God to witness that his intentions to her were honourable, and that he desired nothing more sincerely than that they should marry, and pass their lives together. When he had come to this point, he began to hesitate, as if there was something which he had not courage to utter, till at length he acknowledged with some confusion certain little confidences she had encouraged, and liberties she had allowed. He broke off two or three times in his narration, and assured me most earnestly that he had no wish to make her bad, as he termed it, for he loved her still as sincerely as ever; that the tale had never before escaped his lips, and was only now told to convince me that he was not utterly lost and abandoned. And here, my dear friend, I must commence the old song which you know I utter eternally. If I could only represent the man as he stood, and stands now before me, could I only give his true expressions, you would feel compelled to sympathise in his fate. But enough: you, who know my misfortune and my disposition, can easily comprehend the attraction which draws me toward every unfortunate being, but particularly toward him whose story I have recounted.
CAN: At first the man answered my questions with a quiet sadness that seemed to me a little shy; but soon, as if he'd suddenly recognized himself and me again, more openly he confessed his faults and lamented his misfortune. Dear friend, if only I could put every word of his before you for judgment! He told me, owned to me, even with a kind of relish and happiness in the remembering, that his passion for his mistress had grown in him from day to day, until at last he no longer knew what he was doing, what — as he put it — what to do with himself. He couldn't eat, couldn't drink, couldn't sleep; something choked his throat; he did what he wasn't to do, forgot what he was told; it was as if an evil spirit chased him, until one day, knowing she'd gone up to an upper room, he'd followed her — been drawn after her, rather. When she wouldn't listen to his pleading, he tried to take her by force; he doesn't know what came over him, and God is his witness that his intentions toward her were always honest, that he wished nothing more fervently than that she'd marry him and spend her life with him. When he'd talked a while, he began to stammer, like a man with something more to say he doesn't dare bring out; until at last, shyly, he confessed too what little intimacies she'd allowed him, what nearness she'd granted. He broke off two or three times, and swore again and again that he wasn't saying it to make her out bad, as he put it — that he loved and prized her as before, that it had never crossed his lips, and he was only telling me to show that he wasn't a man wholly turned upside-down and out of his mind. And here, dear friend, I take up the old song again that I'll sing forever: if only I could set the man before you as he stood before me, as he stands before me still! If only I could tell you everything right, so you'd feel how I take part, must take part, in his fate! But enough — since you know my own fate too, know me, you know all too well what draws me to every poor wretch, and to this one above all.
--- para 2 ---
SRC: On perusing this letter a second time, I find I have omitted the conclusion of my tale; but it is easily supplied. She became reserved toward him, at the instigation of her brother who had long hated him, and desired his expulsion from the house, fearing that his sister's second marriage might deprive his children of the handsome fortune they expected from her; as she is childless. He was dismissed at length; and the whole affair occasioned so much scandal, that the mistress dared not take him back, even if she had wished it. She has since hired another servant, with whom, they say, her brother is equally displeased, and whom she is likely to marry; but my informant assures me that he himself is determined not to survive such a catastrophe.
CAN: Reading this letter over, I see I've forgotten to tell the end of the story — but it's easily guessed. She held herself off from him; her brother stepped in, a man who'd long hated him and wanted him out of the house, afraid that a new marriage of his sister's would rob his own children of the inheritance they hope for from her, since she's childless; and this brother threw him out at last, and made such a noise about the whole thing that the woman couldn't have taken him back even if she'd wanted to. Now she's hired another man, and they say she's fallen out with her brother over him too, and people are sure she'll marry this one — and my lad swears he won't live to see it.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: This story is neither exaggerated nor embellished: indeed, I have weakened and impaired it in the narration, by the necessity of using the more refined expressions of society.
CAN: What I'm telling you is not overdone, not prettied up; I may even say I've told it weakly, weakly, and coarsened it by telling it in our decent, handed-down words.
--- para 4 ---
SRC: This love, then, this constancy, this passion, is no poetical fiction. It is actual, and dwells in its greatest purity amongst that class of mankind whom we term rude, uneducated. We are the educated, not the perverted. But read this story with attention, I implore you. I am tranquil to-day, for I have been employed upon this narration: you see by my writing that I am not so agitated as usual. I read and re-read this tale, Wilhelm: it is the history of your friend! My fortune has been and will be similar; and I am neither half so brave nor half so determined as the poor wretch with whom I hesitate to compare myself.
CAN: So this love, this faithfulness, this passion is no poet's invention. It lives, it is there in its purest being among the class of people we call uneducated, call crude. We educated ones — miseducated into nothing! Read the story with reverence, I beg you. Today I'm calm, writing it down; you see by my hand that I'm not scrawling and smearing as usual. Read it, my dear, and remember as you do that it's the story of your friend too. Yes, so it has gone with me, so it will go; and I'm not half so brave, not half so resolute, as the poor wretch — whom I almost don't dare set beside myself.

=== Ch 62: September 5, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Charlotte had written a letter to her husband in the country, where he was detained by business. It commenced, "My dearest love, return as soon as possible: I await you with a thousand raptures." A friend who arrived, brought word, that, for certain reasons, he could not return immediately. Charlotte's letter was not forwarded, and the same evening it fell into my hands. I read it, and smiled. She asked the reason. "What a heavenly treasure is imagination:" I exclaimed; "I fancied for a moment that this was written to me." She paused, and seemed displeased. I was silent.
CAN: She'd written a note to her husband in the country, where he's kept by business. It began, "Dearest, best, come back as soon as ever you can; I wait for you with a thousand joys." A friend who came in brought word that, for certain reasons, he couldn't be back so soon. The note was left lying, and that evening it fell into my hands. I read it and smiled; she asked why. "What a heavenly gift the imagination is," I cried, "that I could fancy for a moment this was written to me." She broke off, seemed displeased, and I fell silent.

=== Ch 63: September 6, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: It cost me much to part with the blue coat which I wore the first time I danced with Charlotte. But I could not possibly wear it any longer. But I have ordered a new one, precisely similar, even to the collar and sleeves, as well as a new waistcoat and pantaloons.
CAN: It cost me a struggle, but at last I had to give up the plain blue coat I wore the first time I danced with Charlotte; toward the end it had got quite past wearing. But I've had one made exactly like it, collar and cuffs and all, and a yellow waistcoat and breeches to match.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: But it does not produce the same effect upon me. I know not how it is, but I hope in time I shall like it better.
CAN: It doesn't do quite the same, though. I don't know — I think in time I'll come to like it better.

=== Ch 64: September 12, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: She has been absent for some days. She went to meet Albert. To-day I visited her: she rose to receive me, and I kissed her hand most tenderly.
CAN: She'd been away a few days to fetch Albert. Today I went into her room; she rose to meet me, and I kissed her hand with such joy.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: A canary at the moment flew from a mirror, and settled upon her shoulder. "Here is a new friend," she observed, while she made him perch upon her hand: "he is a present for the children. What a dear he is! Look at him! When I feed him, he flutters with his wings, and pecks so nicely. He kisses me, too, only look!"
CAN: A canary flew off the mirror and onto her shoulder. "A new friend," she said, and coaxed it onto her hand; "it's for the little ones. Look how sweet it is! When I give it bread it flutters its wings and pecks so prettily. And it kisses me — look!"
--- para 2 ---
SRC: She held the bird to her mouth; and he pressed her sweet lips with so much fervour that he seemed to feel the excess of bliss which he enjoyed.
CAN: She held her mouth to the little creature, and it pressed against her sweet lips so eagerly, as if it could feel the bliss it touched.
--- para 3 ---
SRC: "He shall kiss you too," she added; and then she held the bird toward me. His little beak moved from her mouth to mine, and the delightful sensation seemed like the forerunner of the sweetest bliss.
CAN: "It shall kiss you too," she said, and held the bird across. Its little beak made the way from her mouth to mine, and the pecking touch was like a breath, a foretaste, of love's joy.
--- para 4 ---
SRC: "A kiss," I observed, "does not seem to satisfy him: he wishes for food, and seems disappointed by these unsatisfactory endearments."
CAN: "Its kiss isn't quite without greed," I said; "it's hunting for food, and turns away unsatisfied from an empty caress."
--- para 5 ---
SRC: "But he eats out of my mouth," she continued, and extended her lips to him containing seed; and she smiled with all the charm of a being who has allowed an innocent participation of her love.
CAN: "It eats out of my mouth, too," she said, and offered it a few crumbs from her lips, smiling out of all the joy of an innocent, sharing love.
--- para 6 ---
SRC: I turned my face away. She should not act thus. She ought not to excite my imagination with such displays of heavenly innocence and happiness, nor awaken my heart from its slumbers, in which it dreams of the worthlessness of life! And why not? Because she knows how much I love her.
CAN: I turned my face away. She shouldn't do it! Shouldn't fire my imagination with these pictures of heavenly innocence and bliss, shouldn't wake my heart out of the sleep into which the dullness of life sometimes lulls it! And why not? — She trusts me so! She knows how I love her!

=== Ch 65: September 15, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: It makes me wretched, Wilhelm, to think that there should be men incapable of appreciating the few things which possess a real value in life. You remember the walnut trees at S—, under which I used to sit with Charlotte, during my visits to the worthy old vicar. Those glorious trees, the very sight of which has so often filled my heart with joy, how they adorned and refreshed the parsonage yard, with their wide-extended branches! and how pleasing was our remembrance of the good old pastor, by whose hands they were planted so many years ago: The schoolmaster has frequently mentioned his name. He had it from his grandfather. He must have been a most excellent man; and, under the shade of those old trees, his memory was ever venerated by me. The schoolmaster informed us yesterday, with tears in his eyes, that those trees had been felled. Yes, cut to the ground! I could, in my wrath, have slain the monster who struck the first stroke. And I must endure this!—I, who, if I had had two such trees in my own court, and one had died from old age, should have wept with real affliction. But there is some comfort left, such a thing is sentiment, the whole village murmurs at the misfortune; and I hope the vicar's wife will soon find, by the cessation of the villagers' presents, how much she has wounded the feelings of the neighborhhood. It was she who did it, the wife of the present incumbent (our good old man is dead), a tall, sickly creature who is so far right to disregard the world, as the world totally disregards her. The silly being affects to be learned, pretends to examine the canonical books, lends her aid toward the new-fashioned reformation of Christendom, moral and critical, and shrugs up her shoulders at the mention of Lavater's enthusiasm. Her health is destroyed, on account of which she is prevented from having any enjoyment here below. Only such a creature could have cut down my walnut trees! I can never pardon it. Hear her reasons. The falling leaves made the court wet and dirty; the branches obstructed the light; boys threw stones at the nuts when they were ripe, and the noise affected her nerves; and disturbed her profound meditations, when she was weighing the difficulties of Kennicot, Semler, and Michaelis. Finding that all the parish, particularly the old people, were displeased, I asked "why they allowed it?" "Ah, sir!" they replied, "when the steward orders, what can we poor peasants do?" But one thing has happened well. The steward and the vicar (who, for once, thought to reap some advantage from the caprices of his wife) intended to divide the trees between them. The revenue-office, being informed of it, revived an old claim to the ground where the trees had stood, and sold them to the best bidder. There they still lie on the ground. If I were the sovereign, I should know how to deal with them all, vicar, steward, and revenue-office. Sovereign, did I say? I should, in that case, care little about the trees that grew in the country.
CAN: It's enough to drive a man mad, Wilhelm, that there should be people without sense or feeling for the few things on earth that still have any worth. You remember the walnut trees I sat under with Charlotte at the good vicar's at St—, those glorious trees that, God knows, always filled my soul with the greatest delight! How homely they made the parsonage yard, how cool, and how splendid the branches were; and the memory going back to the good clergymen who planted them so many years ago. The schoolmaster often gave us one of their names, that he'd heard from his grandfather; a fine man he's said to have been, and his memory was always sacred to me under those trees. I tell you, the schoolmaster had tears in his eyes yesterday when we spoke of how they'd been cut down. Cut down! I could go mad, I could kill the dog that struck the first blow. I, who could grieve myself to death if a couple of such trees stood in my yard and one died of old age — I have to stand by and watch this. But there's one thing, dear friend — there is such a thing as human feeling! The whole village grumbles, and I hope the vicar's wife will feel in her butter and eggs and the rest of it what a wound she's dealt the place. For it was she — the new incumbent's wife (our old one has died too) — a lean, sickly thing with good reason to take no part in the world, since no one takes any part in her. A fool who fancies herself learned, dabbles in the canon, works away busily at the newfangled moral-critical reform of Christianity, and shrugs her shoulders at Lavater's raptures; her health quite broken, so that she has no joy in God's earth. Only such a creature could cut down my walnut trees. You see, I can't get over it! Just imagine: the falling leaves make her yard wet and moldy; the trees rob her of daylight; and when the nuts are ripe the boys throw stones at them, and it jars her nerves, disturbs her deep musings as she weighs Kennicott against Semler and Michaelis. When I saw how put out the people were, the old ones especially, I said, "Why did you let it happen?" "When the bailiff wants a thing, here in the country," they said, "what's to be done?" But one thing pleased me. The bailiff and the vicar, who meant at least to make something off his wife's whims — the trees were to be theirs — wanted to split the profit; but the treasury heard of it, said, "This way," and bought the trees, for it had an old claim to that bit of ground. Down they lie, sold. Oh, if I were the prince! I'd know what to do with the vicar's wife, the bailiff, and the treasury — prince! Ah, if I were prince, what would I care about the trees in my own land!

=== Ch 66: October 10, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Only to gaze upon her dark eyes is to me a source of happiness! And what grieves me, is, that Albert does not seem so happy as he—hoped to be—as I should have been—if—I am no friend to these pauses, but here I cannot express it otherwise; and probably I am explicit enough.
CAN: Just to look into her black eyes makes me well! And what galls me is that Albert doesn't seem as happy as he — hoped to be — as I — would have been — if — I'm no friend of these dashes, but here there's no other way to say it; and I think I've said it plainly enough.

=== Ch 67: October 12, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Ossian has superseded Homer in my heart. To what a world does the illustrious bard carry me! To wander over pathless wilds, surrounded by impetuous whirlwinds, where, by the feeble light of the moon, we see the spirits of our ancestors; to hear from the mountain-tops, mid the roar of torrents, their plaintive sounds issuing from deep caverns, and the sorrowful lamentations of a maiden who sighs and expires on the mossy tomb of the warrior by whom she was adored. I meet this bard with silver hair; he wanders in the valley; he seeks the footsteps of his fathers, and, alas! he finds only their tombs. Then, contemplating the pale moon, as she sinks beneath the waves of the rolling sea, the memory of bygone days strikes the mind of the hero, days when approaching danger invigorated the brave, and the moon shone upon his bark laden with spoils, and returning in triumph. When I read in his countenance deep sorrow, when I see his dying glory sink exhausted into the grave, as he inhales new and heart-thrilling delight from his approaching union with his beloved, and he casts a look on the cold earth and the tall grass which is so soon to cover him, and then exclaims, "The traveller will come,—he will come who has seen my beauty, and he will ask, 'Where is the bard, where is the illustrious son of Fingal?' He will walk over my tomb, and will seek me in vain!" Then, O my friend, I could instantly, like a true and noble knight, draw my sword, and deliver my prince from the long and painful languor of a living death, and dismiss my own soul to follow the demigod whom my hand had set free!
CAN: Ossian has driven Homer out of my heart. What a world the glorious bard leads me into! To roam over the heath, the storm-wind raging around, leading the spirits of the fathers in the smoking mist by the dim light of the moon; to hear from the hills, half-drowned in the roar of the mountain torrent, the fading groans of the spirits in their caves, and the laments of the girl grieving herself to death by the four moss-grown, grassy stones of the hero, her beloved, fallen. Then I find him, the gray wandering bard, seeking on the wide heath the footprints of his fathers, and finding, alas, their gravestones, and looking up with a wail to the dear evening star that hides in the rolling sea, and the ages of the past wake to life in the hero's soul, when the kindly beam still lit the brave through their dangers, and the moon shone down on their garlanded, homecoming ship. When I read the deep grief on his brow, see the last forsaken, glorious one stagger weary toward the grave, drinking ever new, painful-glowing joys from the powerless presence of his dead, and looking down at the cold earth, at the tall waving grass, and crying: "The wanderer will come, will come, who knew me in my beauty, and ask, Where is the singer, Fingal's noble son? His step passes over my grave, and he asks for me in vain over the earth." Then, O friend, like a noble squire I'd draw the sword and free my prince at one stroke from the quivering torment of a slow-dying life — and send my own soul after the freed half-god.

=== Ch 68: October 19, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Alas! the void the fearful void, which I feel in my bosom! Sometimes I think, if I could only once but once, press her to my heart, this dreadful void would be filled.
CAN: Oh, this gap, this fearful gap I feel here in my breast! I often think: if you could just once, only once, press her to this heart, the whole gap would be filled.

=== Ch 69: October 26, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Yes, I feel certain, Wilhelm, and every day I become more certain, that the existence of any being whatever is of very little consequence. A friend of Charlotte's called to see her just now. I withdrew into a neighbouring apartment, and took up a book; but, finding I could not read, I sat down to write. I heard them converse in an undertone: they spoke upon indifferent topics, and retailed the news of the town. One was going to be married; another was ill, very ill, she had a dry cough, her face was growing thinner daily, and she had occasional fits. "N— is very unwell too," said Charlotte. "His limbs begin to swell already," answered the other; and my lively imagination carried me at once to the beds of the infirm. There I see them struggling against death, with all the agonies of pain and horror; and these women, Wilhelm, talk of all this with as much indifference as one would mention the death of a stranger. And when I look around the apartment where I now am—when I see Charlotte's apparel lying before me, and Albert's writings, and all those articles of furniture which are so familiar to me, even to the very inkstand which I am using,—when I think what I am to this family—everything. My friends esteem me; I often contribute to their happiness, and my heart seems as if it could not beat without them; and yet—-if I were to die, if I were to be summoned from the midst of this circle, would they feel—or how long would they feel the void which my loss would make in their existence? How long! Yes, such is the frailty of man, that even there, where he has the greatest consciousness of his own being, where he makes the strongest and most forcible impression, even in the memory, in the heart, of his beloved, there also he must perish,—vanish,—and that quickly.
CAN: Yes, it grows certain to me, dear friend, certain and ever more certain, that the being of a single creature counts for little, very little. A friend of Charlotte's came to see her, and I went into the next room to find a book, and couldn't read, and then took up a pen to write. I heard them talking softly; they were telling each other trifles, the town news: this one's getting married, that one is sick, very sick — "a dry cough, the bones standing out in her face, and fainting fits; I wouldn't give a penny for her life," said the one. "And N— is in a bad way too," said Charlotte. "He's all swollen up," said the other. And my lively imagination set me down at the bedside of these poor people; I saw them turn from life with all their reluctance, saw them — and Wilhelm, my little ladies talked of it exactly as one talks of a stranger's dying. And when I look around me and look around this room, and see Charlotte's clothes lying about, and Albert's papers, and the furniture I'm so at home with, even this inkwell, I think: see what you are to this house! All in all. Your friends honor you, you often make their joy, and it seems to your heart it couldn't beat without them — and yet — if you went now, if you left this circle — would they feel, and how long would they feel, the gap your loss tears in their fate? How long? Oh, man is so fleeting that even there, where he has the one sure sense of his own being, where he makes the one true mark — even in the memory, in the soul of those he loves — there too he must be blotted out, must vanish, and that so soon!

=== Ch 70: October 27, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I could tear open my bosom with vexation to think how little we are capable of influencing the feelings of each other. No one can communicate to me those sensations of love, joy, rapture, and delight which I do not naturally possess; and, though my heart may glow with the most lively affection, I cannot make the happiness of one in whom the same warmth is not inherent.
CAN: Often I could tear my own breast open and dash out my brains, that one man can be so little to another. Ah, the love, the joy, the warmth, the rapture I don't bring to it myself, another won't give me, and with a heart full of bliss I can't make a man happy who stands cold and powerless before me.

=== Ch 71: October 27 — Evening, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I possess so much, but my love for her absorbs it all. I possess so much, but without her I have nothing.
CAN: I have so much, and the feeling for her swallows it all up; I have so much, and without her it all comes to nothing.

=== Ch 72: October 30, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: One hundred times have I been on the point of embracing her. Heavens! what a torment it is to see so much loveliness passing and repassing before us, and yet not dare to lay hold of it! And laying hold is the most natural of human instincts. Do not children touch everything they see? And I!
CAN: A hundred times now I've been on the point of throwing my arms around her. The great God knows what it is to see so much loveliness move about before you and not be allowed to reach for it; and reaching is the most natural drive in us. Don't children reach for everything that comes into their heads? — And I?

=== Ch 73: November 3, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Witness, Heaven, how often I lie down in my bed with a wish, and even a hope, that I may never awaken again. And in the morning, when I open my eyes, I behold the sun once more, and am wretched. If I were whimsical, I might blame the weather, or an acquaintance, or some personal disappointment, for my discontented mind; and then this insupportable load of trouble would not rest entirely upon myself. But, alas! I feel it too sadly. I am alone the cause of my own woe, am I not? Truly, my own bosom contains the source of all my sorrow, as it previously contained the source of all my pleasure. Am I not the same being who once enjoyed an excess of happiness, who, at every step, saw paradise open before him, and whose heart was ever expanded toward the whole world? And this heart is now dead, no sentiment can revive it; my eyes are dry; and my senses, no more refreshed by the influence of soft tears, wither and consume my brain. I suffer much, for I have lost the only charm of life: that active, sacred power which created worlds around me,—it is no more. When I look from my window at the distant hills, and behold the morning sun breaking through the mists, and illuminating the country around, which is still wrapped in silence, whilst the soft stream winds gently through the willows, which have shed their leaves; when glorious nature displays all her beauties before me, and her wondrous prospects are ineffectual to extract one tear of joy from my withered heart, I feel that in such a moment I stand like a reprobate before heaven, hardened, insensible, and unmoved. Oftentimes do I then bend my knee to the earth, and implore God for the blessing of tears, as the desponding labourer in some scorching climate prays for the dews of heaven to moisten his parched corn.
CAN: God knows, I lie down so often in bed with the wish, sometimes even the hope, never to wake again; and in the morning I open my eyes, see the sun again, and am wretched. Oh, that I could be moody, lay the blame on the weather, on a third person, on a failed undertaking — then the unbearable weight of discontent would lie only half on me. Woe is me! I feel too surely that the fault lies in me alone — not the fault, no! Enough that the wellspring of all misery is hidden in me, as the wellspring of all bliss once was. Am I not the same man who once swam in a fullness of feeling, who turned every step into a paradise, who had a heart big enough to take in all of loving nature? And this heart is dead now; no raptures flow from it any more; my eyes are dry, and my senses, no longer washed by refreshing tears, knot my brow with anxious furrows. I suffer much, for I've lost what was the one delight of my life — the holy, quickening power with which I made worlds around me; it's gone! When I look out my window now at the far hill, at how the morning sun breaks through the mist above it and lights up the still meadow-ground, and the gentle river winds toward me between its leafless willows — oh, when this glorious nature stands before me as stiff as a little lacquered picture, and all the bliss can't pump one drop of happiness out of my heart up into my brain, and the whole fellow stands before the face of God like a dried-up well, like a cracked bucket! How often I've thrown myself on the ground and begged God for tears, as a farmer begs for rain when the sky is brass above him and the earth around him is dying of thirst.
--- para 1 ---
SRC: But I feel that God does not grant sunshine or rain to our importunate entreaties. And oh, those bygone days, whose memory now torments me! why were they so fortunate? Because I then waited with patience for the blessings of the Eternal, and received his gifts with the grateful feelings of a thankful heart.
CAN: But, ah, I feel it — God doesn't give rain and sunshine to our stormy begging; and those old days, whose memory tortures me — why were they so blessed? Because I waited in patience for his spirit, and took the joy he poured over me with a whole, a thankfully whole heart.

=== Ch 74: November 8, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Charlotte has reproved me for my excesses, with so much tenderness and goodness! I have lately been in the habit of drinking more wine than heretofore. "Don't do it," she said. "Think of Charlotte!" "Think of you!" I answered; "need you bid me do so? Think of you—I do not think of you: you are ever before my soul! This very morning I sat on the spot where, a few days ago, you descended from the carriage, and—" She immediately changed the subject to prevent me from pursuing it farther. My dear friend, my energies are all prostrated: she can do with me what she pleases.
CAN: She's reproached me for my excesses — ah, so sweetly! My excesses: that I let myself be led, sometimes, by a glass of wine, into drinking a bottle. "Don't," she said. "Think of Charlotte!" "Think!" I said. "Need you tell me to? I think — I don't think! You're always before my soul. Today I sat on the spot where you got down from the carriage the other day —" She changed the subject to keep me from going deeper. Dear friend, I'm done for! She can do with me what she likes.

=== Ch 75: November 15, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I thank you, Wilhelm, for your cordial sympathy, for your excellent advice; and I implore you to be quiet. Leave me to my sufferings. In spite of my wretchedness, I have still strength enough for endurance. I revere religion—you know I do. I feel that it can impart strength to the feeble and comfort to the afflicted, but does it affect all men equally? Consider this vast universe: you will see thousands for whom it has never existed, thousands for whom it will never exist, whether it be preached to them, or not; and must it, then, necessarily exist for me? Does not the Son of God himself say that they are his whom the Father has given to him? Have I been given to him? What if the Father will retain me for himself, as my heart sometimes suggests? I pray you, do not misinterpret this. Do not extract derision from my harmless words. I pour out my whole soul before you. Silence were otherwise preferable to me, but I need not shrink from a subject of which few know more than I do myself. What is the destiny of man, but to fill up the measure of his sufferings, and to drink his allotted cup of bitterness? And if that same cup proved bitter to the God of heaven, under a human form, why should I affect a foolish pride, and call it sweet? Why should I be ashamed of shrinking at that fearful moment, when my whole being will tremble between existence and annihilation, when a remembrance of the past, like a flash of lightning, will illuminate the dark gulf of futurity, when everything shall dissolve around me, and the whole world vanish away? Is not this the voice of a creature oppressed beyond all resource, self-deficient, about to plunge into inevitable destruction, and groaning deeply at its inadequate strength, "My God! my God! why hast thou forsaken me?" And should I feel ashamed to utter the same expression? Should I not shudder at a prospect which had its fears, even for him who folds up the heavens like a garment?
CAN: Thank you, Wilhelm, for your warm heart, for your well-meant advice — and be easy. Let me bear it out; for all my weariness I have strength enough to see it through. I honor religion, you know that; I feel it's a staff for many a weary soul, refreshment for many a fainting one. Only — can it be, must it be, that for everyone? When you look at the great world, you see thousands for whom it wasn't, thousands for whom it never will be, whether it's preached to them or not — so must it be that for me? Doesn't God's own Son say that those will be around him whom the Father gave him? And what if I'm not given? What if the Father wants to keep me for himself, as my heart sometimes tells me? — I beg you, don't read this wrong; don't see mockery in these innocent words. It's my whole soul I lay open to you; otherwise I'd rather have kept silent — as I'd rather not waste a word on what no one knows any more about than I do. What is it, the lot of man, but to drink out his measure, to drain his cup to the dregs? And if the cup was too bitter on the lips of the God of heaven in his human body, why should I play the brave man and pretend it tastes sweet? And why should I be ashamed, in the terrible moment when my whole being trembles between being and not-being, when the past flashes like lightning over the dark gulf of the future and everything around me sinks and the world goes down with me? Isn't this the voice of the creature thrown back wholly on itself, failing itself, falling past all holding, groaning down into the depths — "My God, my God, why hast thou forsaken me?" And should I be ashamed of the word, should I dread the moment, when he couldn't escape it who rolls up the heavens like a cloth?

=== Ch 76: November 21, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: She does not feel, she does not know, that she is preparing a poison which will destroy us both; and I drink deeply of the draught which is to prove my destruction. What mean those looks of kindness with which she often—often? no, not often, but sometimes, regards me, that complacency with which she hears the involuntary sentiments which frequently escape me, and the tender pity for my sufferings which appears in her countenance?
CAN: She doesn't see, doesn't feel, that she's brewing a poison that will be the ruin of me and of her; and I, with full pleasure, drain the cup she hands me to my destruction. What does it mean, that kind look she so often — often? no, not often, but now and then — gives me; the readiness with which she takes an involuntary show of my feeling; the pity for what I bear, written on her brow?
--- para 1 ---
SRC: Yesterday, when I took leave she seized me by the hand, and said, "Adieu, dear Werther." Dear Werther! It was the first time she ever called me dear: the sound sunk deep into my heart. I have repeated it a hundred times; and last night, on going to bed, and talking to myself of various things, I suddenly said, "Good night, dear Werther!" and then could not but laugh at myself.
CAN: Yesterday, as I left, she gave me her hand and said, "Adieu, dear Werther!" Dear Werther! It was the first time she called me "dear," and it went through me to the marrow. I've said it over to myself a hundred times, and last night, going to bed and chattering all sorts of things to myself, I suddenly said, "Good night, dear Werther!" — and then had to laugh at my own self.

=== Ch 77: November 22, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I cannot pray, "Leave her to me!" and yet she often seems to belong to me. I cannot pray, "Give her to me!" for she is another's. In this way I affect mirth over my troubles; and, if I had time, I could compose a whole litany of antitheses.
CAN: I can't pray, "Let her be mine!" — and yet she so often seems to be mine. I can't pray, "Give her to me!" — for she belongs to another. I make jokes around my pains; if I let myself go I could spin out a whole litany of opposites.

=== Ch 78: November 24, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: She is sensible of my sufferings. This morning her look pierced my very soul. I found her alone, and she was silent: she steadfastly surveyed me. I no longer saw in her face the charms of beauty or the fire of genius: these had disappeared. But I was affected by an expression much more touching, a look of the deepest sympathy and of the softest pity. Why was I afraid to throw myself at her feet? Why did I not dare to take her in my arms, and answer her by a thousand kisses? She had recourse to her piano for relief, and in a low and sweet voice accompanied the music with delicious sounds. Her lips never appeared so lovely: they seemed but just to open, that they might imbibe the sweet tones which issued from the instrument, and return the heavenly vibration from her lovely mouth. Oh! who can express my sensations? I was quite overcome, and, bending down, pronounced this vow: "Beautiful lips, which the angels guard, never will I seek to profane your purity with a kiss." And yet, my friend, oh, I wish—but my heart is darkened by doubt and indecision—could I but taste felicity, and then die to expiate the sin! What sin?
CAN: She feels what I suffer. Today her look went deep into my heart. I found her alone; I said nothing, and she looked at me. And I no longer saw in her the lovely beauty, no longer the gleam of her splendid spirit; all that had vanished before me. A far more glorious look worked on me, full of the deepest sympathy, the sweetest tenderness. Why couldn't I throw myself at her feet? Why couldn't I fall on her neck and answer with a thousand kisses? She took refuge at the piano and breathed out, in a soft, sweet voice, low melodies in time with her playing. Never have I seen her lips so lovely; it was as if they parted thirstily to drink in the sweet tones welling from the instrument, and only the secret echo sounded back from the pure mouth — ah, if only I could say it to you! I gave way, bowed down, and swore: never will I dare press a kiss on you, lips over which the spirits of heaven hover. And yet — I want to — ha! you see, it stands like a wall before my soul — this bliss — and then to perish, to atone for the sin — Sin?

=== Ch 79: November 26, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: Oftentimes I say to myself, "Thou alone art wretched: all other mortals are happy, none are distressed like thee!" Then I read a passage in an ancient poet, and I seem to understand my own heart. I have so much to endure! Have men before me ever been so wretched?
CAN: Now and then I tell myself: your fate is unique; call the others happy — no one was ever so tormented. Then I read some poet of the old world, and it's as if I were looking into my own heart. I have so much to bear! Ah, have men before me been so wretched?

=== Ch 80: November 30, 1772 ===
--- para 0 ---
SRC: I shall never be myself again! Wherever I go, some fatality occurs to distract me. Even to-day alas—for our destiny! alas for human nature!
CAN: I'll never come to myself again! Wherever I turn I meet some sight that throws me off. Today! O fate! O human kind!
--- para 1 ---
SRC: About dinner-time I went to walk by the river-side, for I had no appetite. Everything around seemed gloomy: a cold and damp easterly wind blew from the mountains, and black, heavy clouds spread over the plain. I observed at a distance a man in a tattered coat: he was wandering among the rocks, and seemed to be looking for plants. When I approached, he turned round at the noise; and I saw that he had an interesting countenance in which a settled melancholy, strongly marked by benevolence, formed the principal feature. His long black hair was divided, and flowed over his shoulders. As his garb betokened a person of the lower order, I thought he would not take it ill if I inquired about his business; and I therefore asked what he was seeking. He replied, with a deep sigh, that he was looking for flowers, and could find none. "But it is not the season," I observed, with a smile. "Oh, there are so many flowers!" he answered, as he came nearer to me. "In my garden there are roses and honeysuckles of two sorts: one sort was given to me by my father! they grow as plentifully as weeds; I have been looking for them these two days, and cannot find them. There are flowers out there, yellow, blue, and red; and that centaury has a very pretty blossom: but I can find none of them." I observed his peculiarity, and therefore asked him, with an air of indifference, what he intended to do with his flowers. A strange smile overspread his countenance. Holding his finger to his mouth, he expressed a hope that I would not betray him; and he then informed me that he had promised to gather a nosegay for his mistress. "That is right," said I. "Oh!" he replied, "she possesses many other things as well: she is very rich." "And yet," I continued, "she likes your nosegays." "Oh, she has jewels and crowns!" he exclaimed. I asked who she was. "If the states-general would but pay me," he added, "I should be quite another man. Alas! there was a time when I was so happy; but that is past, and I am now—" He raised his swimming eyes to heaven. "And you were happy once?" I observed. "Ah, would I were so still!" was his reply. "I was then as gay and contented as a man can be." An old woman, who was coming toward us, now called out, "Henry, Henry! where are you? We have been looking for you everywhere: come to dinner." "Is he your son?" I inquired, as I went toward her. "Yes," she said: "he is my poor, unfortunate son. The Lord has sent me a heavy affliction." I asked whether he had been long in this state. She answered, "He has been as calm as he is at present for about six months. I thank Heaven that he has so far recovered: he was for one whole year quite raving, and chained down in a madhouse. Now he injures no one, but talks of nothing else than kings and queens. He used to be a very good, quiet youth, and helped to maintain me; he wrote a very fine hand; but all at once he became melancholy, was seized with a violent fever, grew distracted, and is now as you see. If I were only to tell you, sir—" I interrupted her by asking what period it was in which he boasted of having been so happy. "Poor boy!" she exclaimed, with a smile of compassion, "he means the time when he was completely deranged, a time he never ceases to regret, when he was in the madhouse, and unconscious of everything." I was thunderstruck: I placed a piece of money in her hand, and hastened away.
CAN: I went down by the water at noon; I had no appetite. Everything was bleak; a cold, wet west wind blew off the mountains, and gray rain-clouds drove into the valley. From a distance I saw a man in a shabby green coat scrambling among the rocks, who seemed to be looking for plants. As I came nearer and he turned at the sound, I saw a very interesting face, whose chief mark was a quiet sadness, but otherwise nothing but an honest, good nature; his black hair was pinned up in two rolls, the rest braided in a thick plait down his back. As his dress seemed to mark him a man of humble station, I thought he wouldn't take it amiss if I noticed what he was at, and so I asked what he was looking for. "I'm looking," he answered with a deep sigh, "for flowers — and find none." "But it isn't the season for them," I said, smiling. "There are so many flowers," he said, coming down to me. "In my garden there are roses and two kinds of honeysuckle, one my father gave me; they grow like weeds; I've been looking for them two days now and can't find them. Out here there are always flowers too, yellow and blue and red, and the centaury has a pretty little blossom. I can't find a one of them." I noticed something uncanny, and so asked, in a roundabout way, what he wanted the flowers for. A strange, twitching smile drew over his face. "If you won't give me away," he said, pressing a finger to his lips, "I promised my sweetheart a posy." "That's good of you," I said. "Oh," he said, "she has lots of other things; she's rich."
