--- para 92 ---
SRC: "Be so good as to lend me your pistols for a journey. Adieu."
CAN: Would you lend me your pistols for a journey I have ahead of me? Keep well!
--- para 93 ---
SRC: Charlotte had slept little during the past night. All her apprehensions were realised in a way that she could neither foresee nor avoid. Her blood was boiling in her veins, and a thousand painful sensations rent her pure heart. Was it the ardour of Werther's passionate embraces that she felt within her bosom? Was it anger at his daring? Was it the sad comparison of her present condition with former days of innocence, tranquillity, and self-confidence? How could she approach her husband, and confess a scene which she had no reason to conceal, and which she yet felt, nevertheless, unwilling to avow? They had preserved so long a silence toward each other and should she be the first to break it by so unexpected a discovery? She feared that the mere statement of Werther's visit would trouble him, and his distress would be heightened by her perfect candour. She wished that he could see her in her true light, and judge her without prejudice; but was she anxious that he should read her inmost soul? On the other hand, could she deceive a being to whom all her thoughts had ever been exposed as clearly as crystal, and from whom no sentiment had ever been concealed? These reflections made her anxious and thoughtful. Her mind still dwelt on Werther, who was now lost to her, but whom she could not bring herself to resign, and for whom she knew nothing was left but despair if she should be lost to him for ever.
CAN: The dear woman had slept little that night; what she'd dreaded was decided, decided in a way she could neither have foreseen nor feared. Her blood, that had always flowed so pure and light, was in a feverish uproar; a thousand feelings tore at her fine heart. Was it the fire of Werther's embraces she felt in her breast? Was it anger at his boldness? Was it a sullen comparing of her present state with those days of wholly free, untroubled innocence and easy trust in herself? How was she to meet her husband, how confess to him a scene she might well confess, and yet didn't dare? They'd kept silent so long with each other — and should she be the first to break it, and at just the wrong moment make her husband so unwelcome a discovery? Already she feared that the mere news of Werther's visit would strike him badly, and now this unlooked-for catastrophe on top of it! Could she really hope her husband would see her in the right light, take it without the least prejudice? And could she wish he should read in her soul? And yet again, could she dissemble before the man to whom she'd always stood as open and clear as glass, from whom she'd never hidden, never could hide, a single feeling? Each thought troubled her, set her at a loss; and again and again her mind came back to Werther, lost to her, whom she couldn't let go, whom — alas — she had to leave to himself, and for whom, once he'd lost her, nothing was left.
--- para 94 ---
SRC: A recollection of that mysterious estrangement which had lately subsisted between herself and Albert, and which she could never thoroughly understand, was now beyond measure painful to her. Even the prudent and the good have before now hesitated to explain their mutual differences, and have dwelt in silence upon their imaginary grievances, until circumstances have become so entangled, that in that critical juncture, when a calm explanation would have saved all parties, an understanding was impossible. And thus if domestic confidence had been earlier established between them, if love and kind forbearance had mutually animated and expanded their hearts, it might not, perhaps, even yet have been too late to save our friend.
CAN: How heavily the deadlock lay on her now — the thing that had settled between her and Albert, which she couldn't even now explain to herself! Such sensible, such good people had begun, over secret differences, to fall silent toward each other, each brooding on his own right and the other's wrong, and the matter so tangled and inflamed that the knot, just at the critical moment when everything hung on it, was impossible to loose. Had a happy openness brought them together sooner again, had love and forbearance come alive between them and opened their hearts, perhaps our friend might still have been saved.
--- para 95 ---
SRC: But we must not forget one remarkable circumstance. We may observe from the character of Werther's correspondence, that he had never affected to conceal his anxious desire to quit this world. He had often discussed the subject with Albert; and, between the latter and Charlotte, it had not unfrequently formed a topic of conversation. Albert was so opposed to the very idea of such an action, that, with a degree of irritation unusual in him, he had more than once given Werther to understand that he doubted the seriousness of his threats, and not only turned them into ridicule, but caused Charlotte to share his feelings of incredulity. Her heart was thus tranquillised when she felt disposed to view the melancholy subject in a serious point of view, though she never communicated to her husband the apprehensions she sometimes experienced.
CAN: There was one more strange thing in it. Werther had never made a secret, as we know from his letters, that he longed to leave this world. Albert had often argued against it, and Charlotte and her husband had sometimes spoken of it together. He, with his decided dislike of the act itself, had often shown, with a kind of touchiness quite unlike him, that he found great cause to doubt the seriousness of such a purpose; he'd even made a joke of it now and then, and passed his unbelief on to Charlotte. This eased her heart when her thoughts turned to the sad picture, but on the other hand it kept her from sharing with her husband the fears that tormented her just then.
--- para 96 ---
SRC: Albert, upon his return, was received by Charlotte with ill-concealed embarrassment. He was himself out of humour; his business was unfinished; and he had just discovered that the neighbouring official with whom he had to deal, was an obstinate and narrow-minded personage. CAN: Albert came home, and Charlotte went to meet him with a hurried embarrassment; he was out of humor, his business unfinished; the neighboring official had shown himself a stubborn, narrow man. The bad road, too, had soured him on the way.
--- para 97 ---

CAN: He asked whether anything had happened, and she answered too quickly that Werther had been there the evening before. He asked whether any letters had come, and was told a letter and some packages lay in his room. He went in, and Charlotte was left alone.
--- para 98 ---
SRC: The presence of the being she loved and honoured produced a new impression on her heart. CAN: The presence of the man she loved and honored had made a new impression on her heart. She found him busy breaking the seals and reading. Some of the contents seemed less than pleasant. She asked a few questions; he answered shortly and went to his desk to write.
--- para 99 ---
SRC: Several hours passed in this manner, and Charlotte's feelings became more and more melancholy. She felt the extreme difficulty of explaining to her husband, under any circumstances, the weight that lay upon her heart; and her depression became every moment greater, in proportion as she endeavoured to hide her grief, and to conceal her tears.
CAN: They were together that way for an hour, and Charlotte's mood grew darker and darker. She felt how hard it would be to make clear to her husband, even in the best of moods, what lay on her heart; and she sank into a sadness all the more frightening because she tried to hide it and to swallow her tears.
--- para 100 ---
SRC: The arrival of Werther's servant occasioned her the greatest embarrassment. He gave Albert a note, which the latter coldly handed to his wife, saying, at the same time, "Give him the pistols. I wish him a pleasant journey," he added, turning to the servant. These words fell upon Charlotte like a thunderstroke: she rose from her seat half-fainting, and unconscious of what she did. She walked mechanically toward the wall, took down the pistols with a trembling hand, slowly wiped the dust from them, and would have delayed longer, had not Albert hastened her movements by an impatient look. She then delivered the fatal weapons to the servant, without being able to utter a word. As soon as he had departed, she folded up her work, and retired at once to her room, her heart overcome with the most fearful forebodings. She anticipated some dreadful calamity. She was at one moment on the point of going to her husband, throwing herself at his feet, and acquainting him with all that had happened on the previous evening, that she might acknowledge her fault, and explain her apprehensions; then she saw that such a step would be useless, as she would certainly be unable to induce Albert to visit Werther. Dinner was served; and a kind friend whom she had persuaded to remain assisted to sustain the conversation, which was carried on by a sort of compulsion, till the events of the morning were forgotten.
CAN: The appearance of Werther's boy threw her into the greatest distress. He handed Albert the note, and Albert turned coolly to his wife and said, "Give him the pistols." — "I wish him a good journey," he said to the boy. It struck her like a thunderbolt; she rose, swaying; she didn't know what was happening to her. Slowly she went to the wall, took down the weapons trembling, wiped the dust from them, hesitated, and would have put it off longer if Albert hadn't pressed her with a questioning look. She gave the boy the unhappy things without being able to get out a word, and when he'd gone she gathered up her work and went to her room, in a state past all telling. Her heart foretold every horror. Now she was on the point of throwing herself at her husband's feet, confessing everything, the scene of the evening before, her guilt and her forebodings. Then again she saw no good could come of it; least of all could she hope to talk her husband into going to Werther. The table was laid; a good friend, who'd only come to ask something and meant to go at once, stayed — and made the talk at table bearable; they forced themselves, chatted, told stories, forgot.

--- para 107 ---
SRC: "Past eleven o'clock! All is silent around me, and my soul is calm. I thank thee, O God, that thou bestowest strength and courage upon me in these last moments! SRC: The old steward hastened to the house immediately upon hearing the news: he embraced his dying friend amid a flood of tears. His eldest boys soon followed him on foot.
